The backbone itself stores no backward activations. Inference also avoids saved backward state, although live forward intermediates and workspaces still consume memory. Adapters distributed through the network create a different topology because trainable parameters sit inside early layers as well as the final layer. Even when a base weight matrix is frozen, the backward pass must still propagate upstream activation gradients through the intervening base layers to reach earlier adapters. Setting requires_grad=False on base weights avoids computing or storing gradients for those frozen weights and lets a frozen linear projection skip saving its forward input solely for a base-weight update. However, the forward pass must still record the computational graph across the backbone and retain the activations needed to differentiate every intervening nonlinearity, normalization, attention operation, and adapter branch along that path. Disabling gradient recording across the backbone would sever the backward path and leave earlier adapters with zero gradient.

↩︎ Gradient-recording and detachment controls for frozen feature extractors are established in Saved tensors and the memory-compute trade-off.

The comparison therefore separates a residency decision from a differentiation decision. Subtracting \(\WeightMemory + \GradientMemory + \OptimizerStateMemory\) from device capacity gives the space available for activations and runtime overhead. A head-only configuration may fit where full fine-tuning does not, provided it meets the task’s quality target; an adapter configuration must still budget the activations on its backward path. If those activations exceed the remaining space, checkpointing or a smaller micro-batch may still be necessary despite the small optimizer state. For a fixed training scope, only activation memory scales approximately linearly with batch size (equation 3); model parameters and optimizer state remain fixed. The Llama 3 8B case puts numbers on both decisions.

Napkin Math 8.4: Fine-tuning Llama 3 8B on one H100
Problem: Can a 8.0B-parameter Llama 3 model be fine-tuned on one H100 with 80 GB (85.9 GB in decimal units) of HBM, first with every parameter trained and then with low-rank adapters?

Given: The mixed-precision Adam recipe of section 8.3.2, a batch of 4 sequences of 2,048 tokens, and attention that never stores the quadratic score matrix.

Math:

  1. Full fine-tuning, static state: FP16 weights 16 GB + FP16 gradients 16 GB + FP32 master weights 32 GB + Adam moments 64 GB = 128 GB, already beyond one accelerator before any activation is stored.
  2. Activations: The per-layer estimate of section 8.3.3.1, without the quadratic attention term, gives 36.5 GB at this batch, so full fine-tuning needs about 165 GB in all.
  3. Low-rank adapters: Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) (Hu et al. 2021) freezes the base weights and trains a pair of small matrices beside each adapted projection. Rank-16 adapters on every query and value projection add 6.8 million trainable parameters, 0.08 percent of the model, whose gradients, master copies, and moments occupy 0.11 GB.
  4. Adapter total: Frozen BF16 weights 16 GB + adapter state 0.11 GB + activations 36.5 GB ≈ 53 GB, leaving about 33 GB for workspaces and allocator overhead.

Systems insight: Full-parameter training of this model does not fit one accelerator; it requires partitioning its state across devices with fully sharded data parallelism (FSDP) or ZeRO, which section 8.7.1.1 develops, or offloading state to host memory. Adapters change which terms of the memory budget are populated: the frozen weights stay resident, but gradients and optimizer state shrink to the adapters, and activations still follow the backward path through every frozen layer. Storing the frozen base weights in a 4-bit format shrinks the largest remaining term further (Dettmers et al. 2023), a recipe distinct from the full-parameter mixed-precision training above.

Dettmers, Tim, Artidoro Pagnoni, Ari Holtzman, and Luke Zettlemoyer. 2023. “QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 10088–115.
Hu, Edward J., Yelong Shen, Phillip Wallis, Zeyuan Allen-Zhu, Yuanzhi Li, Shean Wang, Lu Wang, and Weizhu Chen. 2021. “LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2106.09685.

Forward propagation, gradient computation, and parameter updates define what training systems must compute. An operation’s name alone, however, does not reveal where the system stalls. Matrix shape, kernel fusion, numerical precision, and the memory hierarchy determine whether arithmetic throughput or data movement binds execution. Arithmetic intensity provides the analytical tool to quantify that balance.

8.3.4 Arithmetic intensity

During a single training step on the reference H100, a large dense matrix multiplication runs near the chip’s Tensor Core peak while a standalone softmax or LayerNorm kernel stalls waiting for bytes from HBM. Arithmetic intensity explains that split by comparing the operations a kernel performs to the bytes it moves across the memory bus: \[ \ArithmeticIntensity = \frac{\Operations}{\DataVolume} \]

Within the roofline model, operations above the hardware ridge point have a compute-throughput ceiling, while those below it have a memory-bandwidth ceiling. Launch overhead, limited parallelism, and imperfect execution can keep measured performance below either ceiling. Performance Bounds develops the roofline model and the hardware ridge point, and The Roofline model works through the derivation.12

12 Ridge point and precision: The roofline ridge point, the arithmetic intensity threshold separating memory-bound from compute-bound operations, shifts with numerical precision. On the same accelerator, a lower-precision Tensor Core path can expose much more arithmetic throughput at roughly the same memory bandwidth, raising the ridge point substantially. Switching from TF32-style execution to BF16 mixed precision can therefore change which optimization technique yields returns, making precision selection inseparable from roofline analysis.

↩︎ Arithmetic intensity, the ridge point, and the memory-bound and compute-bound regimes they separate are established informally in Matching workloads to hardware.

Table 5 summarizes common tendencies rather than fixed measurements. Dense matrix multiplication can reuse operands enough to reach high arithmetic intensity, while standalone elementwise and reduction kernels often perform less work per byte moved. The target hardware’s ridge point determines the resulting regime.

Hardware efficiency during model training depends directly on kernel arithmetic intensity. Figure 1 bounds attainable throughput across training kernels on the H100’s roofline, contrasting memory-bandwidth-bound operators (softmax, LayerNorm) along the sloped ceiling against compute-bound operators (dense MatMul) on the flat 989 TFLOP/s peak.

On the reference machine the ridge in figure 1 sits at 295.2 FLOP/byte, the H100’s 989 TFLOP/s peak divided by its 3.35 TB/s of HBM bandwidth. The operation points in this diagram are illustrative placements that show how to interpret the model, not measured GPT-2 coordinates.

Consider a GPT-2 attention layer that materializes the \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention-score matrix in HBM. For each head, the \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) product performs \(\SequenceLength^2\AttentionHeadDimension\) multiply-accumulates, or \(2\SequenceLength^2\AttentionHeadDimension\) FLOPs. Writing the resulting \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) FP16 score matrix to HBM (2 bytes per element) and reading it back for the subsequent softmax kernel (another 2 bytes per element) moves \(4\SequenceLength^2\) bytes. Dividing \(2\SequenceLength^2\AttentionHeadDimension\) FLOPs by \(4\SequenceLength^2\) bytes cancels \(\SequenceLength^2\) and leaves an arithmetic intensity of \(\AttentionHeadDimension/2\) FLOP/byte. For GPT-2 Small (\(\HiddenDimension=\) 768 across 12 heads, so \(\AttentionHeadDimension=\) 64), this restricted accounting yields 32 FLOP/byte. The lighthouse GPT-2 XL gives the same answer, because its head dimension is also 64 and the ratio depends only on \(\AttentionHeadDimension\). A complete kernel analysis must also count Q and K traffic, softmax work, additional reads and writes, cache reuse, and fusion. The \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) materialized score traffic remains the term that FlashAttention targets.

At 295.2 FLOP/byte, the H100’s ridge sits far to the right of the 32 FLOP/byte materialized attention estimate, so low-intensity operations such as attention softmax remain memory bound even when large matrix multiplications on the same device are compute bound.

Systems Perspective 8.2: Peak FLOP/s vs. sustained performance
Peak TFLOP/s is an arithmetic ceiling rather than a prediction of sustained application performance. For an implementation that is strictly bandwidth bound, raising only the arithmetic ceiling does not improve the roofline bound. Mixed-precision training can both enable faster arithmetic paths and reduce traffic for tensors actually stored and transferred at lower precision. The realized gain depends on kernel support, conversion overhead, numerical requirements, and which resource is binding. Roofline-guided optimization therefore measures the implementation before choosing fusion, precision changes, or additional compute.

Batch size also moves an operation along the intensity axis, because a larger batch spreads each weight load over more examples. Where the transition from memory-bound to compute-bound execution falls depends on the operation’s shape, its precision, and the hardware.

This analysis guides optimization strategy selection. For memory-bound operations, reducing data movement through kernel fusion, reduced precision, or algorithmic improvements like FlashAttention provides the largest gains. For compute-bound operations, increasing throughput through Tensor Cores and parallel execution matters more. The first case asks how to move fewer bytes, and the second asks how to keep more arithmetic units busy.

The trajectory vector in figure 1 illustrates how FlashAttention13 can shift transformer attention toward, and for favorable shapes across, the 295.2 FLOP/byte ridge line onto the compute ceiling. By never materializing the full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention matrix in HBM and instead processing tiles that fit in fast SRAM (tiling, the general technique developed in Tiling), FlashAttention reduces auxiliary attention memory from \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) to \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) and sharply reduces HBM traffic. The original work reports up to 3\(\times\) speedups on evaluated workloads (Dao et al. 2022), with gains depending on tensor shape, hardware, and baseline. The algorithm and the conditions under which it applies are examined in detail in section 8.6.4.

13 FlashAttention: The core mechanism processes attention in small tiles that fit within the accelerator’s fast on-chip SRAM, avoiding writes of the full intermediate \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) matrix to slower HBM. The exact HBM IO bound depends on tile size and SRAM capacity, but the practical effect is a large reduction in memory traffic and auxiliary activation storage.

Arithmetic-intensity analysis is how the silicon contract (principle 4) is checked in training. The silicon contract names the resource a workload is built to exploit; the analysis determines which physical resource actually constrains a particular implementation. Dense matrix multiplication reaches the compute-bound regime when tensor shapes provide sufficient data reuse, while standalone normalization and elementwise activation kernels remain memory bandwidth bound.

Optimizing individual operations is necessary but insufficient. A perfectly tuned matrix multiplication achieves nothing if the accelerator sits idle waiting for the next batch of data. Isolated operation costs (dense matrix multiplication FLOPs, memory-bound activations, and optimizer state allocations) compound within the execution loop. Sustained throughput requires orchestrating these discrete operations into a balanced pipeline where upstream data delivery and downstream parameter updates prevent accelerator starvation.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why do batched matrix-matrix multiplications (GEMM, BLAS Level 3) usually achieve much higher accelerator utilization in neural network training than matrix-vector operations (GEMV, BLAS Level 2)?

    1. Matrix-matrix operations avoid gradient computation during backpropagation, so they save both memory traffic and arithmetic work.
    2. Under the ideal traffic model in the section, GEMM reuses loaded operands across many outputs, so arithmetic intensity can grow with matrix size, whereas GEMV offers much less reuse and tends to stay near constant arithmetic intensity.
    3. Matrix-vector operations cannot run efficiently on GPUs because every layer result must be synchronized back to the CPU before the next layer starts.
    4. Batched matrix-matrix operations reduce the model’s parameter count, which is why they keep more of the network in HBM.

  2. A team trains a 7-billion-parameter model on accelerators with 80 GB of HBM each. Weights, gradients, activations, and common workspaces already occupy 64 GB per accelerator, and this total already includes any FP32 master weights if the recipe uses them. The model is unsharded per device. Using the section’s optimizer-memory accounting, explain the systems trade-off between choosing plain SGD and Adam for this run.

  3. The point on a roofline model curve where the memory-bandwidth-bound diagonal ceiling intersects the flat peak-compute ceiling, defined mathematically as \(\text{Peak FLOP/s} / \text{Memory Bandwidth}\), is known as the ____ point.

  4. Order the following events in a standard backpropagation training step to reflect their strict causal and data dependencies:

  1. Compute scalar objective loss by evaluating predictions against ground-truth labels.
  2. Update model parameters using gradient-based optimization rules (such as Adam or SGD).
  3. Execute forward propagation through successive layers while caching intermediate activations.
  4. Evaluate the chain rule backward from the loss through layers to compute parameter and activation gradients.
  5. Fetch and preprocess the training mini-batch on the host and transfer it to device memory.

  1. For a transformer with hidden dimension \(d_{\text{model}} = 768\) and 12 heads (\(d_{\text{head}} = 64\)), the section’s restricted accounting gives the materialized attention-score computation an arithmetic intensity of about \(d_{\text{head}}/2 = 32\text{ FLOP/byte}\). If the target accelerator is the reference H100, whose ridge point is about \(295\text{ FLOP/byte}\), what does that imply and what optimization direction best matches the section?

    1. The kernel is compute-bound, so increasing peak TFLOP/s is the main expected lever under this model.
    2. The kernel is latency-bound, so shrinking the batch is the main way to move it onto the compute roof.
    3. The kernel is network-bound, so the right first response is a faster inter-node AllReduce fabric.
    4. The kernel falls on the memory-bandwidth side of the roofline under this accounting, so reducing materialized score traffic through IO-aware tiling is the appropriate optimization direction.

8.4 Pipeline Architecture

A training step is not a single operation but a sequence of dependent stages: data must be loaded before computation can begin, forward passes must complete before backward passes start, and gradients must be computed before parameters can update. The speed of the slowest stage determines the speed of the entire system. Without pipelining, these stages serialize, idling the accelerator while the host prepares data or transfers batches across the bus.

While model execution graphs govern arithmetic on the accelerator, sustained training requires feeding those kernels across physical machine boundaries. Host CPUs ingest raw records from storage and apply preprocessing in host DRAM, while accelerators execute matrix multiplications in HBM. Data must cross the interconnect, typically a PCIe link whose bandwidth is an order of magnitude lower than device memory bandwidth. When upstream data preparation or bus transfer exceeds the duration of the forward and backward passes, the accelerator compute engines starve, reducing the hardware utilization factor \(\EfficiencyHw\) in the training-time law.

The training system divides into three subsystems. The data pipeline handles ingestion, transformation, and batching on the host; the training loop coordinates forward passes, backward passes, and parameter updates on the accelerator; and the evaluation pipeline periodically assesses model quality on held-out data. The three subsystems decouple across the training lifecycle (figure 2): processed batches flow into the core forward/backward training loop while metrics stream to an asynchronous evaluation pipeline. The interfaces between them form critical synchronization boundaries where I/O latency, interconnect transfers, or synchronous validation pauses can throttle accelerator utilization. The data pipeline supplies two of the three terms of the pipeline bottleneck model defined later (equation 6), host preprocessing and host-to-accelerator transfer. The accelerator training loop supplies the third through matrix compute, and periodic evaluation claims shared execution time from that loop.

8.4.1 Pipeline stages and accelerator bubbles

A training step passes through four stages, a staged system pipeline rather than a monolithic loop. If one stage is slower than the rest, the others wait:

  1. Data Loading & Preprocessing (CPU/Storage): Fetching raw bytes from Non-Volatile Memory Express (NVMe) storage, decoding samples, and executing transformations on CPU cores.
  2. Host-to-Device Transfer (PCIe): Moving the preprocessed batch across the PCIe bus into GPU memory via direct memory access (DMA).
  3. Forward/Backward Pass (Accelerator): Propagating activations forward and gradients backward through layer kernels on the GPU.
  4. Parameter Synchronization (Interconnect, present only when multiple accelerators train the model): Exchanging gradients between accelerators across the available interconnect fabric. NVLink provides up to 900 GB/s in the H100 configuration examined later, while alternative architectures rely on PCIe switches or scale-out network fabrics.

Any throughput mismatch among these stages creates accelerator bubbles, idle windows during which compute units stall while waiting for upstream dependencies. These bubbles stem from imbalances within the per-step pipeline. A systems engineer eliminates them through asynchronous prefetching and pipeline overlapping, staging the next batch into high-bandwidth memory before the active step completes. The first three stages form the analytical basis for the pipeline execution model in equation 6, where the slowest stage dictates end-to-end iteration throughput, and section 8.5 provides the profiling methods required to isolate that bottleneck.

Those per-step stages map directly onto the subsystem boundaries of figure 2. At the ingestion boundary, host workers decode, normalize, and augment raw records (LeCun et al. 2012) before packing dense batches for PCIe transfer, while at the validation boundary, periodic evaluation on held-out data competes for the same accelerator cores and trades monitoring resolution against training throughput. Within the accelerator itself, the training iteration forms a closed feedback loop governed by strict data and memory dependencies. Figure 3 traces this cyclic sequence across three stages (predict, gradients, and update), showing how predictions, the loss, and parameter gradients feed the optimizer before returning updated weights to the next iteration.

LeCun, Yann, Leon Bottou, Genevieve B. Orr, and Klaus-Robert Müller. 2012. “Efficient BackProp.” In Neural Networks: Tricks of the Trade, vol. 7700. Lecture Notes in Computer Science. Springer Berlin Heidelberg. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-642-35289-8_3.

While section 8.3 counted the FLOPs and bytes of each operator in this cycle, memory residency and data dependencies govern how the hardware executes them. During the forward pass, intermediate activation tensors must remain resident in accelerator HBM because the backward pass requires them to evaluate chain-rule derivatives. Once gradients are computed, the optimizer reads both the parameters and accumulated gradients to apply the update rule, producing new weights for the subsequent iteration. The memory capacity required to hold activations, weights, and optimizer states establishes the memory floor for the entire training step.

This execution loop repeats across batches and epochs until the optimization converges. Maximizing accelerator arithmetic efficiency requires overlapping these pipeline stages across the system hierarchy. Host CPU threads prefetch and stage batch \(\BatchIndex+1\) into page-locked (pinned) host memory while the accelerator executes batch \(\BatchIndex\), so the DMA engine can stream it across PCIe asynchronously (Devices and memory explains why pinning is required). This concurrent staging hides storage latency and bus transfer time behind matrix-multiplication compute kernels. When this concurrency breaks down, because an I/O-bound data pipeline starves the device of input tensors or frequent evaluation stalls the update loop, the accelerator idles, degrading effective throughput.

8.4.2 Data pipeline

Keeping an accelerator’s arithmetic units saturated requires streaming training examples from persistent storage into device memory at or above the model’s consumption rate. Data engineering posed this as the feeding problem (The feeding problem) and measured the throughput lost when a slow storage path cannot keep up; training is where that loss is paid on every step. ResNet-50 is the chapter’s input-pipeline case, because its images are far heavier for the host to prepare than GPT-2’s token IDs. The stage trace below starts with GPT-2 XL under a scenario assumption, on-the-fly single-core tokenization (production runs usually pre-tokenize), and the system implications then turn to ResNet-50. Upstream data engineering prepares and partitions datasets on storage media, while the runtime data pipeline executes concurrently with model computation on the host CPU.

The data pipeline bridges raw storage and accelerator execution across the machine boundary. Figure 4 partitions this data movement across three physical zones: staging raw files from persistent storage into host memory, formatting and augmenting tensors across CPU worker threads, and streaming batched inputs over the interconnect to parallel GPU accelerators.

In the storage zone, raw records reside on local NVMe drives or networked storage systems. In the CPU preprocessing zone, host worker threads decode raw bytes, apply transformations such as tokenization or data augmentation, and collate individual records into dense mini-batch tensors. In the GPU training zone, accelerators transfer those tensors across the PCIe bus and execute forward and backward passes. Because these three zones form a strict pipeline, the slowest stage dictates end-to-end training throughput. In the pipeline model developed below, the storage and CPU zones together set the preprocessing term, the PCIe crossing sets the transfer term, and the GPU zone sets the compute term.

Core components

Data pipeline throughput originates at the storage subsystem. Upstream layout decisions, such as sequential chunking in columnar or container formats (Parquet, TFRecord, Arrow), dictate the physical I/O patterns presented to the operating system.

↩︎ How file format and storage layout set the data term of the iron law is established in Storage tiers and cost.

Storage throughput is bounded by the slower of two hardware constraints, \(\StorageThroughput= \min(\DiskBandwidth, \NetworkBandwidth)\), where \(\DiskBandwidth\) is the physical disk bandwidth and \(\NetworkBandwidth\) represents the network bandwidth for distributed storage systems. In practice, training workloads rarely achieve this theoretical maximum because data shuffling can turn large sequential reads into smaller, less efficient accesses. Effective storage throughput can be modeled as \(\EffectiveStorageThroughput= \StorageThroughput\times \StorageAccessFactor\), where \(\StorageAccessFactor\) depends on record size, file layout, caching, and storage hardware. When unbuffered random reads degrade \(\StorageAccessFactor\), storage bandwidth drops by an order of magnitude, transforming an I/O system that appears sufficient on paper into a primary training bottleneck.

GPT-2’s 40 GB WebText corpus fits in the host memory of a single training server, so once cached it can be served from system RAM without repeated storage reads, although preprocessing and transfer costs remain. A corpus that exceeds local memory must instead be streamed, and the system must orchestrate storage reads, preprocessing, caching, and delivery throughout training. Whether multiple workers, prefetching, local caches, or distributed storage pay off then depends on which of those stages limits throughput.

Preprocessing

Once retrieved from storage, raw bytes require CPU transformation into model-ready tensors. While static feature extraction can run offline, dynamic transformations such as on-the-fly tokenization and image augmentation execute during training. Under ideal independent-worker scaling, the CPU worker pool delivers transformation capacity according to equation 5: \[\PreprocessingThroughput= \frac{\WorkerCount}{\WorkerTransformTime} \tag{5}\] where \(\WorkerCount\) parallel processing threads each perform transformations requiring \(\WorkerTransformTime\) seconds per batch. Training architectures employ multiple workers to ensure transformation keeps pace with accelerator consumption rates.

However, transforming records assumes that storage delivers raw bytes fast enough to keep worker queues filled. Under overlapped host staging, the achieved host preparation rate cannot exceed the slower of storage retrieval and CPU transformation. Dividing effective storage throughput \(\EffectiveStorageThroughput\) by the raw bytes retrieved per batch yields the storage delivery rate in batches per second. When storage delivery lags behind worker demand, additional CPU threads sit idle waiting on disk I/O, capping host throughput regardless of \(\WorkerCount\). The complete host preparation rate is therefore bounded by the lesser of the storage delivery rate and the worker transformation capacity \(\WorkerCount / \WorkerTransformTime\).

Preprocessed data must then transfer across the host-accelerator interconnect before computation can begin. End-to-end training throughput is consequently constrained by the slowest of three sequential stages, as formalized in equation 6. In this three-stage pipeline model, the host preparation rate \(\PreprocessingThroughput\) captures the joint bound of storage delivery and CPU worker transformation, while the remaining terms capture host-to-device bus transfer and device execution: \[\TrainingThroughput=\min\left(\PreprocessingThroughput, \frac{\HostTransferBandwidth}{\BatchDataVolume}, \frac{\SustainedComputeRate}{\BatchOperations}\right) \tag{6}\] where \(\HostTransferBandwidth\) is the host-to-accelerator transfer bandwidth, \(\BatchDataVolume\) is the input bytes per batch transferred to the device, and \(\BatchOperations\) is the forward-backward work per batch in FLOPs. Every term is therefore expressed in batches per second before the minimum is taken.

Example 8.1: GPT-2 language model data pipeline
Scenario: Quantifying storage access, CPU tokenization, and PCIe host-to-device transfers supplying one H100 training GPT-2 XL at batch 32. The device service interval is the step time the training-time law gives at the chapter’s utilization, about 596 ms per batch; fitting that batch in memory requires the techniques of section 8.6, but the input path does not depend on which ones are used.

Stage trace:

  1. Raw text storage: The WebText corpus contributes about 40 GB of raw text. Sequential reads from NVMe SSD storage reach 7 GB/s. Under document sampling with access factor 0.1, random-access storage bandwidth drops to 0.70 GB/s, giving \(\StorageReadTime= \DataVolume / \EffectiveStorageThroughput\approx 0.1\text{ ms}\).
  2. Tokenization: A BPE tokenizer (50,257 vocabulary) converts text into token IDs. For batch size \(\BatchSize=32\) and sequence length \(\SequenceLength=1024\) (32.8K tokens total), a single CPU core processing 50K tokens/s takes \(\TokenizationTime\approx\) 655.4 ms per batch.
  3. Batching and padding: Padding and packing generate an int64 tensor of shape 32 sequences \(\times\) 1,024 tokens, producing 262.1 KB per batch.
  4. PCIe transfer: Transferring the tensor across the H100’s PCIe Gen5 x16 link (64 GB/s bandwidth) takes \(\BatchTransferTime=\) 0.004 ms, making bus transfer negligible.

Systems insight: Single-core CPU preparation (\(\HostPreparationTime \approx\) 655.4 ms) exceeds the accelerator service interval (\(\AcceleratorBatchTime \approx\) 596 ms), so a serial pipeline pays both costs on every step and the device service stage occupies only about 48 percent of the serial step. Slower preparation increases the exposed input wait. Scaling to 8 parallel worker processes reduces the ideal average interval between prepared batches to \(\TokenizationTime / \WorkerCount \approx\) 81.9 ms. Prefetching also overlaps \(\HostPreparationTime\) with \(\AcceleratorBatchTime\) to keep the device stage supplied throughout ideal steady-state execution, delivering 54 sequences per second on one H100. The service interval is a modeled step time, not a measurement of hardware busy time. Keeping inputs available does not establish useful FLOP utilization, which requires a separate useful-work count and measured step time. Parallel workers need not shorten the latency of an individual batch, and contention or imperfect overlap can reduce the modeled throughput.

This min-of-three relationship governs training pipeline design because system throughput equals bottleneck throughput. An accelerator with 989 TFLOP/s of compute capacity delivers zero useful work while waiting for data. Conversely, a perfectly optimized data pipeline provides no benefit if the accelerator is already compute-saturated. Balanced pipeline design aligns preprocessing capacity, transfer bandwidth, and compute throughput so that no single stage dominates iteration time. Under that scenario, the GPT-2 workload shows CPU tokenization, rather than storage retrieval or bus transfer, forming the primary throughput barrier unless parallel workers and prefetching conceal host preparation latency.

Data pipeline throughput is not the only flow that can starve the accelerator. Multi-GPU training adds a second stream of traffic: the gradient synchronization required after every step. Once synchronization time exceeds compute time, communication becomes the limiting factor. Section 8.7.2 quantifies that network wall at the point where training crosses the node boundary.

System implications

Comparing supply with demand requires both in the same unit. Grouping the two host-side terms of equation 6 into a single pipeline rate \(\InputPipelineRate\), in batches per second, and writing the accelerator’s consumption rate as \(\AcceleratorConsumptionRate= \SustainedComputeRate/\BatchOperations\), also in batches per second (where \(\SustainedComputeRate\) stays in FLOP/s), leaves a coupled system whose throughput is \(\min(\InputPipelineRate, \AcceleratorConsumptionRate)\). When \(\InputPipelineRate< \AcceleratorConsumptionRate\), the accelerator sits idle waiting for data, and its busy-time fraction drops proportionally in this ideal supply model, as equation 7 shows. Busy time counts every moment the accelerator is executing, so it is an upper bound on, and distinct from, the model-FLOP utilization defined later in the chapter: \[\text{Accelerator Utilization} = \min\left(1, \frac{\InputPipelineRate}{\AcceleratorConsumptionRate}\right) \times 100\% \tag{7}\]

ResNet-50 shows how quickly that drop arrives on current hardware. One training image costs about 24.6 GFLOP, so the H100’s dense 16-bit peak could consume about 40,200 images/s. A well-tuned training step sustains only part of that ceiling; at the 25 percent fraction implied by the 3,200 images/s that The feeding problem assumed for the previous-generation A100, the H100 consumes about 10,100 images/s. A host worker that decodes and augments 375 images/s would need 28 workers per accelerator to keep up, but an eight-accelerator H100 server provides 112 host cores, 14 per accelerator. Those workers supply about 5,250 images/s, so by equation 7 the accelerator is busy only about 52 percent of the time. The same host fed an A100 with 9 workers and cores to spare. Upgrading the accelerator raised its appetite 3× without changing the host, which moves ResNet-50 from its home compute-bound regime into an input-bound one. Faster arithmetic adds nothing until the input pipeline is rebalanced, with more host cores per accelerator, decoding on the accelerator, or cached decoded data, and section 8.6.2 takes up the overlap that hides what remains.

Data flows

Training data traverses three memory tiers on its way from disk to accelerator, and the bandwidth gap between these tiers, spanning roughly two orders of magnitude, is the central challenge of data pipeline design. For equal byte volumes at each tier, the effective transfer rate is bounded by \(\min(\StorageBandwidth, \SystemMemoryBandwidth, \AcceleratorMemoryBandwidth)\), the same minimum as equation 6 applied to bandwidth tiers. When decoding, tokenization, or reuse changes the bytes handled per batch, each bandwidth must first be divided by its own per-batch byte volume so that the rates are comparable.

Bandwidth steps up the storage to DRAM to HBM hierarchy.

Local NVMe storage provides 7 GB/s, system memory delivers 50 GB/s, and the H100’s HBM delivers 3350 GB/s. Each tier is roughly an order of magnitude or more faster than the one below it, which means data that flows freely within accelerator memory creates a severe bottleneck when it must be fetched from disk. Stated in time rather than rate, the pipeline model covers the case where stages overlap correctly, fetching the next batch from storage while preprocessing the current one and transferring the previous one to the accelerator. In that case the steady-state input service interval, denoted here by iteration time, is governed by the maximum of the stage times, \(\PipelineIterationTime= \max(\BatchFetchTime, \BatchProcessingTime, \BatchTransferTime)\), rather than their sum. This overlap is what prefetching seeks to achieve, with each tier operating concurrently on different batches. End-to-end training must also include accelerator compute and any exposed synchronization or evaluation time.

Practical architectures

Sustaining that stage overlap across the storage-to-HBM gap is harder in practice because an NVMe drive rated at 7 GB/s sustains only 3.50 GB/s at half of peak, and small random reads during dataset shuffling degrade throughput further depending on access size, queue depth, file layout, and caching. To keep accelerators fed despite this bandwidth reduction, pipeline architectures maintain multiple data buffers simultaneously: prefetch buffers loading future batches, processing buffers holding data under transformation, and transfer buffers staging data for accelerator consumption. The total host memory required scales with the per-batch memory footprint \(\BatchBufferMemory\) according to equation 8: \[\PipelineBufferMemory= (\PrefetchBufferCount+ \ProcessingBufferCount+ \TransferBufferCount) \times \BatchBufferMemory \tag{8}\]

To avoid starving the accelerator, the average preprocessing service time per batch across all workers must not exceed the compute time per batch (\(\PreprocessingServiceTime\leq \IronLawComputeTime\)), which restates the preprocessing term of equation 6 in time. If preprocessing takes 40 ms per batch on one worker while the accelerator consumes a batch every 10 ms, ideal scaling requires at least four workers. Actual worker requirements depend on storage throughput, CPU contention, and transformation cost, which section 8.6.2 examines through prefetching and parallel workers.

8.4.3 Forward and backward pass

The operation counts in section 8.3.1.1 become a scheduling problem once a prepared batch reaches the forward pass: the runtime must launch kernels with enough parallel work, stage their operands, and keep the values required by backward execution.

Compute operations

A dense layer’s FLOP count is already fixed by its input width, output width, and batch size. Its execution time also depends on how that work fills the accelerator. A layer with dimensions of \(512 \times 1024\) at batch 64 supplies 33.6 million MACs, or 67.1 MFLOP; the kernel must divide this work among execution units without leaving a long, mostly empty final wave.

Accelerators execute these parallel workloads by grouping execution threads into fixed-size units: warps14 of 32 threads on NVIDIA architectures, or wavefronts of 64 threads on AMD architectures. Hardware execution efficiency depends on aligning tensor dimensions and tile shapes with the thread-block geometry and the operand matrix dimensions required by specialized units like Tensor Cores.

14 Warp: From textile weaving, where a “warp” is the set of threads held taut on a loom while a shuttle weaves across them. NVIDIA adopted the term because GPU threads within a warp execute the same instruction in lockstep, analogous to parallel threads moving together on the loom. An NVIDIA warp contains 32 threads.

Systems Perspective 8.3: Wave quantization and tail effects
A common mistake in ML systems is ignoring the fixed execution granularity of GPU kernels. GPU execution is quantized into waves of work, but the relevant unit is the kernel’s thread or tile mapping, not necessarily one sample per thread.

Warps make the granularity concrete. In the illustrative special case where one independent work item maps to one thread, 32 items fill one warp while 33 items require a second, mostly empty warp. Neural-network kernels usually map each sample to many threads, so batch size alone does not determine this utilization.

On a large GPU such as the NVIDIA H100 with 132 Streaming Multiprocessors (SMs), where each SM schedules groups of warps, the hardware processes thousands of threads in a single wave. If the total workload slightly exceeds a wave boundary (for example, 1.01 waves), the hardware must execute a second, nearly empty wave to completion before the next task begins.

Table 6 quantifies that one-item-per-thread illustration. It is not a general batch-size performance model.

Systems insight: The table teaches the shape of the penalty, not its size for any real kernel. The actionable rule is to choose batch, sequence, and hidden dimensions that are multiples of the kernel’s tile size and that divide into whole waves across the SMs, then benchmark the neighboring candidates. Profiling reveals partially filled warps or waves; batch size alone cannot.

Beyond dense matrix multiplication, distinct neural architectures introduce specialized computational patterns. Convolutional networks, for instance, slide spatial filters across multichannel input tensors. Consider an input tensor of dimensions \(64 \times 224 \times 224 \times 3\) (batch size \(\times\) height \(\times\) width \(\times\) channels) processed by \(7 \times 7\) kernels. Each position requires 147 multiply-accumulate operations, and with 64 filters operating across \(218 \times 218\) spatial dimensions, the arithmetic work reaches hundreds of millions of multiply-accumulate operations per image. Accelerators execute these spatial convolutions by either lowering them into dense GEMMs through column unfolding (im2col) or applying Winograd minimal filtering, which reduces multiplications at the cost of extra additions and transform workspace.

The other operator families impose different scheduling constraints. Attention alternates batched matrix products with row-wise reductions, while normalization requires statistics before normalized outputs can be produced. The arithmetic-intensity analysis in section 8.3.4 explains their traffic cost; the runtime must additionally respect these reduction dependencies and choose which intermediates can remain inside a fused kernel.

Optimized operator libraries such as cuDNN (Chetlur et al. 2014) implement multiple computational algorithms for each layer type, dynamically selecting among them based on tensor dimensions, scratchpad memory limits, and hardware capabilities. For example, cuDNN chooses between direct convolution, GEMM-based lowering, fast Fourier transform (FFT) convolution, and Winograd transforms by evaluating the trade-off between workspace memory overhead and arithmetic operations (NVIDIA Corporation 2023a). These hardware utilization patterns reinforce the coupling between batch size and hardware efficiency established in section 8.3.2.1: larger batch sizes saturate accelerator compute units and amortize memory transfers, but increase intermediate activation storage.

Chetlur, Sharan, Cliff Woolley, Philippe Vandermersch, Jonathan Cohen, John Tran, Bryan Catanzaro, and Evan Shelhamer. 2014. “cuDNN: Efficient Primitives for Deep Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1410.0759.
NVIDIA Corporation. 2023a. NVIDIA cuDNN Developer Guide, Version 8.9.6.

Memory management

Memory management binds during the forward pass, when intermediate activations must be retained in high-bandwidth memory for gradient computation during backpropagation. How quickly that retained state grows depends on the architecture, as a convolutional network shows.

Consider ResNet-50 processing images at 224 \(\times\) 224 resolution with a batch size of 32. The initial convolutional layer produces activation maps of dimension 112 \(\times\) 112 \(\times\) 64; per image at single-precision (4 bytes), this requires approximately 3.2 MB. For an illustrative capacity budget, assume retained forward activations total 8 GB, the backward pass adds another 4 GB of activation working set (a supplied scenario assumption, distinct from stored parameter gradients), and model parameters consume 102.2 MB. This 12.1 GB subtotal excludes parameter gradients, optimizer state, and additional runtime workspace; those terms must be added before deciding whether training fits.

Under the same linear-scaling assumption, doubling the batch size to 64 doubles activation storage: forward activations reach 16 GB and the backward activation working set reaches 8 GB, raising this subtotal to 24.1 GB. In vision models with large spatial resolutions, activations dominate the memory footprint while parameter storage (102.2 MB) is negligible. For large language models, the balance inverts: GPT-3 (175B parameters) requires approximately 700 GB for parameters alone in FP32 (350 GB in FP16), exceeding single-accelerator capacity before allocating any activation memory or optimizer state.

During backpropagation (section 8.3.3), the backward pass traverses that network in reverse order, consuming retained forward activations to compute parameter and activation gradients at roughly twice the arithmetic cost of the forward pass. Memory requirements shift dynamically across layers as activations are freed and gradient buffers are populated. In ResNet-50, mid-network layers expand to 256 filters from the initial 64, where reduced spatial map dimensions partly compensate for higher channel depth, so the peak memory footprint reflects retained forward activations, active gradient buffers, temporary workspaces, and the runtime allocation schedule rather than the width of a single layer alone.

Because accelerator memory (80 GB on the reference H100) must hold those peak activations, parameters, gradients, and optimizer states simultaneously, memory capacity rather than compute throughput frequently caps the feasible batch size. Two system-level techniques analyzed in section 8.6 relieve this capacity bottleneck. Activation checkpointing discards intermediate activations during the forward pass and recomputes them during the backward pass, trading redundant arithmetic for reduced storage, while mixed-precision training halves storage per value for tensors represented in 16-bit formats instead of FP32 (leaving FP32 master weights and optimizer state accounted for separately in the memory budget). Once the backward sweep finishes releasing activations and populating the parameter gradients, execution reaches the parameter update boundary.

8.4.4 Parameter updates

Once backward execution populates the gradient buffers, the optimizer consumes them and updates the persistent state defined in algorithm 1. The important pipeline boundary is the weight mutation: the next forward pass must observe the completed update. Listing 2 shows that boundary at the framework interface.

The optimizer’s memory traffic is exposed on this dependency path. The FP32 Adam update, which moves 28 bytes per parameter in section 8.3.2.4, must finish before the next forward pass reads those weights. Fusing moment updates and weight writes can shorten this interval by reducing passes over HBM, even when the forward and backward matrix products already use the arithmetic units efficiently.

Batch size and parameter updates

Increasing batch size improves accelerator utilization by raising the arithmetic intensity of matrix multiplications and reducing synchronization frequency, but it also alters optimization dynamics. Scaling batch size without retuning the learning-rate schedule can degrade convergence, creating an optimization gap where hardware efficiency comes at the expense of model quality. Figure 5 isolates this dynamic across synthetic loss trajectories: holding the learning rate fixed while expanding from batch 32 to batch 256 leaves an acute optimization gap (dashed silver curve), whereas linearly scaling the learning rate brings convergence close to the baseline path (green curve).

For a fixed token budget, doubling the global batch size halves the number of parameter updates per epoch. To traverse a comparable distance in parameter space with fewer updates, the linear scaling rule of the large-batch ResNet-50 recipe in section 8.3.2.1.2 scales the learning rate proportionally with batch size (\(\ScaledLearningRate = \BaseLearningRate \times \ScaledBatchSize/\BaseBatchSize\)). Although effective across moderate batch ranges when combined with learning-rate warmup, linear scaling does not guarantee equivalent convergence or generalization. Beyond a critical batch size, gradient noise is largely eliminated, and further batch expansion yields diminishing returns per training sample while escalating memory pressure.

Beyond convergence dynamics, batch size dictates communication frequency in distributed systems. A larger global batch reduces the number of optimizer steps and gradient synchronizations per epoch. Because the communication payload of dense all-reduce synchronizations depends on parameter count rather than batch size, larger batches amortize interconnect latency over more compute FLOPs. In distributed settings, local micro-batch size and global batch size jointly determine the maximum degree of data parallelism. When memory capacity constrains the local micro-batch, gradient accumulation (section 8.6.5) decouples the effective batch size from per-device activation memory, accumulating gradients across multiple forward-backward passes before triggering an optimizer step.

8.4.5 Training state and checkpoints

A run that takes 35 days on one accelerator does not run uninterrupted. Hardware fails, jobs are preempted, and numerical instabilities corrupt weights, so a training system must save the run’s state to storage at intervals and restore it exactly. That saved state is a different mechanism from the activation checkpointing of section 8.6.5.1.2, which recomputes values inside one step; a training checkpoint is a file that carries the run across steps.

Resuming requires restoring model weights and optimizer state in lockstep: first-moment vectors, second-moment variance estimates, and step counters. In the mixed-precision Adam recipe of section 8.3.2.3, a checkpoint holds the FP16 working weights (2 bytes per parameter), the FP32 master weights (4 bytes), and the two FP32 moments (8 bytes), 14 bytes per parameter. The FP16 gradients, which every step recomputes, are left out of the 16 bytes per parameter that training holds in device memory. For GPT-2 XL the checkpoint payload is therefore about 21 GB (3 GB + 6 GB + 12 GB), 7× the FP16 weights alone, and writing it to a local NVMe drive at 7 GB/s takes about 3 s. Checkpoint volume governs how long a save stalls the run and how long recovery takes, connecting fault tolerance to the iron law’s \(\DataVolume\) term.

At the framework level, optimizers expose internal state dictionaries via state_dict() for serialization. Listing 3 demonstrates accessing optimizer state dictionaries, and listing 4 shows saving and restoring model parameters and optimizer states. Reproducing the training trajectory also requires the relevant random-number state, data position, learning-rate scheduler, and gradient-scaler state.

A checkpoint holds only persistent state. During a training step, the FP16 gradients rejoin that state in device memory and the saved activations from reverse-mode automatic differentiation sit on top of it, so device memory must hold more than the checkpoint file suggests. Notebook 8.5 works through the full comparison for a billion-parameter model.

Napkin Math 8.5: Training memory beyond the checkpoint
The frameworks chapter’s ResNet-50 breakdown (Recording the backward graph) already showed training memory several times the size of the stored weights. The following scenario extends the comparison to gradients, optimizer state, and saved activations at billion-parameter scale.

Problem: Calculate the memory that gradients, optimizer states, and forward activations add beyond raw model weights in this billion-parameter training scenario.

Math:

  1. Model weights (FP16): 2 GB.
  2. Gradients (FP16): 2 GB (same size as weights).
  3. Master weights (FP32): 4 GB, the full-precision copy that mixed-precision training updates.
  4. Optimizer states (Adam): 8 GB (momentum and variance in FP32, 4\(\times\) the FP16 weight memory).
  5. Activations: For a batch size of 32 and a 100-layer network, reverse-mode automatic differentiation retains the operation-dependent values required by derivative rules; activation checkpointing can omit selected values and recompute them during the backward pass. \[ \ActivationMemory \approx \BatchSize \times \LayerCount \times \SequenceLength \times \HiddenDimension \times \LayerSavedTensorCount\times \ElementStorageSize \] For a 1024-token sequence, 1024-wide hidden state, 1 saved tensor per layer, and \(\ElementStorageSize = 2\) bytes for FP16: \(32 \times 100 \times 1024 \times 1024 \times 1 \times 2 \approx \text{\textbf{6.7}}\,\text{GB}\). Materialized attention terms can add a separate \(\BatchSize \times \AttentionHeadCount \times \SequenceLength^2\) component, which is why memory-efficient attention kernels matter.

Systems insight: A 2 GB model carries 16 GB of steady-state training state once gradients and optimizer buffers have been allocated, 8× its FP16 weights (16 bytes per parameter in the mixed-precision Adam recipe), while its checkpoint file holds 14 bytes per parameter. During a training step, batch-dependent activations add another 6.7 GB, raising the peak to about 20.7 GB beyond the weights. During training, data movement includes saving and retrieving these activations.

Checkpoint cadence decides how much work a failure discards. Because every update depends on the one before it, a single unmitigated numerical explosion does not cost one step; it can invalidate every step taken since the last good checkpoint, which at frontier scale means thousands of accelerator-hours.

War Story 8.1: The PaLM loss spikes (2022)
Context: PaLM’s 540-billion-parameter run used 6,144 TPU v4 chips across two 3,072-chip Pods connected through Google’s data-center network and suffered severe training-instability spikes absent from smaller runs (Chowdhery et al. 2022).

Failure mode: The training loss spiked roughly 20 times despite gradient clipping. The spikes occurred at highly irregular intervals, sometimes late in training, never appeared in smaller models, and had no identified principled mitigation.

Impact: Each recovery discarded recent progress on an expensive 6,144-chip run, so reproducible checkpoints and repeatable data order were prerequisites for replay.

Response: Engineers restarted training from an uncorrupted checkpoint roughly 100 steps prior to each spike and skipped approximately 200–500 data batches around the failure window.

Systems lesson: Every step discarded in a recovery is paid for again in cluster time, so operational fault tolerance decides how much of the hardware’s time turns into lasting progress. Automated loss-spike detection, checkpoint cadence, and batch skipping are core system mechanisms for keeping that lost fraction small.

PaLM’s recoveries kept 6,144 chips busy on steps that were later thrown away, so the cluster’s time was spent without the run advancing. In the training-time law that discarded work appears as a lower utilization factor \(\EfficiencyHw\), which makes checkpoint cadence a throughput decision as well as a reliability one. Frequent saves stall the run for the write time above, while sparse saves lengthen the replay after each failure.

Decomposing the training pipeline exposes the mechanics of data loading, forward and backward execution, parameter updates, and saved training state, while the computation and memory costs in section 8.3 quantify the FLOPs, memory footprint, and bandwidth each phase demands. Yet knowing how stages execute does not reveal where a specific training workload stalls. A pipeline is only as fast as its slowest stage. In an overlapped pipeline where data loading takes 120 ms and computation takes 100 ms, a 20 percent faster kernel saves nothing, because the step still takes 120 ms. Before applying optimization techniques, systems engineers must profile the execution timeline to determine whether compute, memory bandwidth, storage I/O, or network synchronization binds performance.

Checkpoint 8.2: Costing a step and building its pipeline

The first half of the chapter costed one training step and assembled the pipeline that runs it. Check that each piece can be computed before moving on to diagnosis and optimization.

Cost of a step

Pipeline

Self-Check: Question

  1. Which set of subsystems defines the chapter’s high-level training system architecture, and what systems engineering advantage does this decomposition provide?

    1. Storage controller, compiler intermediate representation, and runtime execution engine; this separates hardware target code generation from storage layout.
    2. Data pipeline, training loop, and evaluation pipeline; this separates distinct resource profiles (CPU/storage I/O, accelerator compute/memory, and periodic validation) so bottlenecks can be isolated at subsystem interfaces.
    3. Tokenizer, hyperparameter optimizer, and model registry; this organizes the model deployment lifecycle around developer interfaces.
    4. Gradient aggregator, parameter server, and checkpoint restorer; this decomposes cloud service microservices.

  2. In a profiled training pipeline, CPU data preprocessing sustains \(4\text{ GB/s}\) on the same logical batch representation that host-to-device transfer and GPU consumption are being compared against. Host-to-device PCIe transfer sustains \(32\text{ GB/s}\), and GPU compute consumes data at an equivalent rate of \(12\text{ GB/s}\). Under the section’s ideal-overlap pipeline model, what determines end-to-end throughput, and what is the best first engineering action?

    1. The average of the three rates, so the effective throughput is \(16\text{ GB/s}\) and all stages should be tuned evenly.
    2. The PCIe transfer rate, because every batch must cross the bus before any compute can happen.
    3. The minimum rate, \(4\text{ GB/s}\) from preprocessing, so the first action is to reduce the input-side bottleneck before tuning faster stages.
    4. The GPU compute rate, because training pipelines are ultimately limited by accelerator execution speed rather than host stages.

  3. Explain why CPU-side tokenization and data augmentation can severely bottleneck GPU training even when the resulting tensor transfer across PCIe takes less than one millisecond.

  4. True or False: Scaling the training batch size by \(8\times\) while keeping the learning rate and schedule unchanged guarantees the same model quality in less wall-clock time.

  5. To enable fast, asynchronous Direct Memory Access (DMA) transfers from host RAM to GPU memory without intermediate CPU staging copies, host memory buffers must be allocated as page-locked or ____ memory.

  6. In the chapter’s staged system pipeline framework, what constitutes an “accelerator bubble” and what is the primary diagnostic step to resolve it?

    1. An intentional synchronization barrier inserted by automatic differentiation to ensure memory coherence across gradient passes; resolved by disabling graph checks.
    2. An on-chip SRAM cache eviction event caused by large transformer sequence lengths; resolved by switching to smaller embedding tables.
    3. A loss of arithmetic intensity when batch sizes fall below the hardware ridge point; resolved by forcing FP64 accumulation.
    4. Silicon idleness where accelerator ALUs wait for data loading, host-to-device PCIe transfer, or parameter synchronization; resolved by profiling the four pipeline stages to identify and overlap the slowest stage.

8.5 Identifying Bottlenecks

An accelerator can report near-100 percent activity in nvidia-smi while spending half its cycles stalled on memory traffic, padding, or recomputing discarded activations. Knowing what each operation costs on paper does not show which resource actually saturates during a run, and raw device busy-time percentages cannot separate useful training arithmetic from overhead. Model FLOPs utilization (MFU)15 supplies that comparison by measuring achieved model arithmetic against the hardware’s peak rate.

15 Model FLOPs utilization (MFU): The PaLM paper (Chowdhery et al. 2022) defined MFU as observed throughput divided by the theoretical maximum throughput at peak FLOPs. Its 540-billion-parameter run reported 46.2 percent MFU on 6,144 TPU v4 chips. The remainder is not credited by the model-FLOP numerator, and MFU alone does not identify how much came from memory traffic, communication, pipeline bubbles, recomputation, or other work.

Chowdhery, Aakanksha, Sharan Narang, Jacob Devlin, Maarten Bosma, Gaurav Mishra, Adam Roberts, Paul Barham, et al. 2022. “PaLM: Scaling Language Modeling with Pathways.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2204.02311.
Definition 8.3: Model FLOPs utilization (MFU)

Model FLOPs utilization (MFU) is the efficiency metric \(\ModelFlopsUtilization = \ModelStepOperations/ (\PeakRate \cdot \TrainingStepTime)\), where \(\ModelStepOperations\) is the useful per-step model FLOP count (forward plus backward, excluding rematerialization) and \(\TrainingStepTime\) is the measured wall-clock time per training step. It expresses what fraction of peak hardware throughput is doing useful model computation.

  1. Significance: MFU makes the effective-throughput term in the iron law concrete. For GPT-2 XL (1.5B parameters) processing 32,768 tokens per step on an H100 (989 TFLOP/s dense FP16/BF16 Tensor Core peak), a 0.60 s step gives model FLOPs divided by the available peak-rate FLOP budget of approximately 0.50 (50 percent). The remaining 50 percent is not credited as model FLOPs and can reflect memory traffic, communication, bubbles, non-model operations, or idle time. Diagnosing that gap requires a profile rather than a universal threshold.
  2. Distinction: Hardware utilization reports whether device engines are active, whereas MFU credits the analytical model-FLOP estimate used in its numerator. It can therefore exclude recomputation and padding even when those operations keep the device busy. Comparisons across accelerators remain sensitive to the selected peak-throughput specification and FLOP-counting convention.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that 100 percent MFU is a realistic sustained target. Memory traffic, communication, and non-matrix work keep end-to-end runs below peak. Reported values depend on the model, precision, batch shape, hardware, and MFU definition, and can improve with FlashAttention and careful tuning.

Three distinct metrics are commonly referred to as “utilization” in training systems, and each addresses a different operational question. The hardware efficiency factor \(\EfficiencyHw\) in the training-time law (equation 1) represents the end-to-end fraction of theoretical peak compute converted into useful model arithmetic across an entire run. MFU measures this same ratio over an individual training step, as the direct empirical estimator for \(\EfficiencyHw\). In contrast, the accelerator busy-time fraction of equation 7, the metric reported by standard operating-system and vendor monitoring utilities such as nvidia-smi, indicates only whether execution cores are active, without distinguishing useful matrix products from memory-stall cycles, padding overhead, or rematerialization. Consequently, busy-time utilization serves only as an upper bound on MFU: a data-starved pipeline has a low busy-time fraction, whereas a well-fed but memory-stalled workload shows near-100 percent busy-time utilization alongside low MFU.

Training bottlenecks can be investigated through three categories aligned with the D·A·M taxonomy (Data, Algorithm, Machine). Table 7 connects each axis to a common training bottleneck, its observable symptoms, and relevant optimization techniques. The axis names where the remedy usually lives rather than what the bottleneck is. Compute-bound and memory-bound execution are both mismatches between an algorithm and a machine, as the ridge point showed. Yet a compute-bound kernel is most often relieved by asking for less or cheaper arithmetic (Algorithm), a memory-bound kernel by changing how the machine stages and moves bytes (Machine), and an input stall by repairing the data path (Data). These are diagnostic starting points, not exclusive mappings.

↪ The full D·A·M diagnostic framework, with its troubleshooting matrix and scorecard, is collected for reference in The D·A·M Taxonomy.

Among these three regimes, data-bound stalls are the most frequently misdiagnosed because they manifest on the accelerator as idle gaps rather than saturated execution units. When host-side ingestion, decoding, or preprocessing cannot sustain the throughput demanded by device memory buses, the accelerator starves.

Example 8.2: The GIL-locked GPU
Scenario: An engineering team builds a PyTorch data-loading pipeline using standard Python threading for CPU-bound image preprocessing that holds the global interpreter lock (GIL).

Diagnosis: The GIL serializes these Python-level preprocessing threads, making host preparation time (\(\HostPreparationTime\approx 250 \text{ ms}\)) far exceed GPU execution time (\(\AcceleratorBatchTime\approx 12 \text{ ms}\)), reducing the accelerator busy-time fraction to about 4.6 percent in this serial preparation-and-compute model. Here the fraction measures time spent computing, not arithmetic efficiency relative to peak throughput.

Systems lesson: Python CPU work that holds the GIL can serialize threads and starve GPUs; native operations may release it. Process workers (num_workers > 0) avoid interpreter serialization, while the NVIDIA Data Loading Library (DALI) can move eligible work off the host.

In the execution timeline captured by figure 6, wide white intervals separate brief GPU stream activity bursts, marking periods where accelerator compute engines idle while CPU-side Python threads assemble the next batch.

Two classes of tools expose these signatures. Framework-level profilers record a timeline of operations, memory allocations, and input-pipeline stalls, which locates idle gaps and attributes them to host work. System- and kernel-level GPU profilers record kernel execution, memory transfers, synchronization points, and per-kernel counters such as achieved memory throughput and arithmetic-unit occupancy, which explains why a kernel that runs is slow. The method below uses the first class to find where time goes and the second to explain it.

↪ How profilers and benchmark harnesses turn these measurements into reproducible results is developed in Training Benchmarks.

A disciplined profiling workflow proceeds top-down from system timelines to kernel-level performance counters, mapping observable profiler traces to the D·A·M taxonomy in table 7. First, timeline visualization locates idle intervals between kernel invocations. Correlating these gaps with batch preparation, transfers, host dispatch, and synchronization establishes what prevents the next kernel from running. When input starvation is confirmed, remedies include asynchronous prefetching, multi-process workers, or offloading preprocessing to the accelerator to overlap data movement with compute. Second, when kernels execute contiguously without host-induced gaps, memory throughput relative to peak bandwidth distinguishes memory-bound from compute-bound execution. If HBM traffic saturates while arithmetic units idle, the workload is memory-bound; kernel fusion and memory-efficient attention relieve this constraint by retaining intermediate tensors in on-chip SRAM rather than writing them back to global memory. Finally, if tensor cores operate near theoretical capacity and memory buses have spare bandwidth, the workload is compute-bound, where throughput gains require lower numerical precision, such as BF16 or FP8, to increase peak arithmetic throughput.

Applied to the chapter’s two running cases, the method gives two different first moves. Before profiling anything, the FP32 GPT-2 XL step at batch 32 does not fit in an H100’s memory, a capacity limit that the memory techniques below must address first. Once it fits, the timeline shows the input gaps. With single-core tokenization, host preparation takes about 655.4 ms per batch against a device step of about 596 ms (example 8.1). A serial pipeline therefore keeps the device stage busy only about 48 percent of each step, a Data-axis diagnosis that no kernel optimization can fix. ResNet-50 on the H100 reaches the same axis by a different route: the host cores available per accelerator supply too few decoded images, leaving the accelerator busy only about 52 percent of the time. A diagnosis names the binding resource; it does not remove it. These two profiles, read against the training-time law and the memory constraint, determine which intervention to apply first and which ones would change nothing.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why is Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) a more reliable metric than raw GPU busy percentage (reported by tools like nvidia-smi) when evaluating training systems efficiency?

    1. MFU is measured directly from host CPU clock cycles, eliminating GPU driver instrumentation overhead.
    2. MFU is mathematically fixed to 100% on any healthy accelerator cluster regardless of software overhead.
    3. MFU counts only the theoretical forward and backward FLOPs required by the model architecture divided by the product of peak hardware throughput and measured step time, whereas raw GPU busy percentage also counts time spent on recomputation, padding, and memory stalls outside that model-FLOP numerator.
    4. MFU measures training loss convergence speed per dollar rather than floating-point operations.

  2. An engineer profiles two training workloads. Workload A shows 92 percent GPU busy time, near-saturated HBM bandwidth, low CPU activity, and continuous kernel timelines. Workload B shows 20 percent GPU busy time with regular 50 ms idle gaps, low HBM bandwidth, and 100 percent CPU utilization. Apply the D·A·M taxonomy to classify each workload’s likely bottleneck and state the next optimization category for each.

  3. Order the steps of the systematic training optimization workflow recommended in the chapter:

  1. Classify the primary performance constraint (Data-, Memory-, or Compute-bound) using the D·A·M taxonomy.
  2. Run a representative training run with framework and system profilers enabled to capture CPU, PCIe, and GPU timeline traces.
  3. Re-profile the system to evaluate MFU improvement and identify whether the bottleneck has shifted to a new pipeline stage.
  4. Apply a targeted optimization technique specifically addressing the identified binding constraint.

  1. An engineering team suspects that an individual custom LayerNorm kernel has poor arithmetic intensity and low register occupancy, while another team suspects their DataLoader is causing host-to-device PCIe transfer stalls.     1. A kernel-level GPU profiler with per-kernel counters for the arithmetic intensity and occupancy analysis; a framework-level or system-level timeline profiler for the DataLoader and PCIe transfers.
    2. nvidia-smi for kernel-level arithmetic intensity; TensorBoard loss curves for DataLoader PCIe transfer stalls.

  2. A team’s profiler shows the GPU at 18 percent utilization with periodic drops to zero, while one CPU core is pinned near 100 percent and the PCIe link is mostly idle. Using the section’s staged-pipeline framework, explain how to diagnose the bubble’s source and what fix should be targeted.

8.6 Pipeline Optimizations

Even well-designed training pipelines rarely approach hardware peak throughput without targeted optimization: at a typical model-FLOP utilization of 30 percent to 50 percent, an H100 rated at 989 TFLOP/s delivers only 296.7 TFLOP/s–494.5 TFLOP/s of useful model arithmetic. These derived scenario values reflect the gaps between peak and realized throughput reported across published large-model systems (Narayanan et al. 2021; Chowdhery et al. 2022). Systems such as SuperNeurons show one version of this problem, in which memory-management bottlenecks prevent larger networks from training efficiently unless the runtime actively optimizes activation storage and transfer (Wang et al. 2018). Closing that utilization gap requires matching a targeted systems intervention to the specific resource that profiling shows is saturated: data movement, arithmetic throughput, or memory capacity. Table 8 extends the D·A·M bottleneck classification of table 7 by matching each bottleneck to the technique that removes it, the term of the training-time law that the technique moves, and its effect on memory, which decides feasibility rather than appearing in the law.

Narayanan, Deepak, Mohammad Shoeybi, Jared Casper, Patrick LeGresley, Mostofa Patwary, Vijay Korthikanti, Dmitri Vainbrand, et al. 2021. “Efficient Large-Scale Language Model Training on GPU Clusters Using Megatron-LM.” Proceedings of the International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–15. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458817.3476209.
Wang, Linnan, Jinmian Ye, Yiyang Zhao, Wei Wu, Ang Li, Shuaiwen Leon Song, Zenglin Xu, and Tim Kraska. 2018. “SuperNeurons: Dynamic GPU Memory Management for Training Deep Neural Networks.” Proceedings of the 23rd ACM SIGPLAN Symposium on Principles and Practice of Parallel Programming, 41–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/3178487.3178491.

Because training throughput is governed by whichever resource currently binds the step, applying a remedy from table 8 to a nonbinding subsystem yields no end-to-end speedup, while relieving the active bottleneck immediately shifts pressure to the next stage in the Data, Algorithm, and Machine triad (the conservation-of-complexity heuristic from the opening of Part I). Even as the governing constraint shifts with system scale, from host-ingestion stalls on a 1 GB vision model to HBM capacity and attention bandwidth on GPT-2 XL, every optimization pass follows the same diagnostic loop.

8.6.1 Systematic optimization framework

Tuning a training pipeline without a profile wastes work on resources that are not limiting step time or shifts the bottleneck to a neighbor. A disciplined optimization pass therefore proceeds in three steps: profile the step to locate the binding constraint, pick an intervention that relieves that specific limit, and check how combined interventions compete for shared memory and bandwidth.

First, profiling tools such as PyTorch Profiler, TensorFlow Profiler, or NVIDIA Nsight Systems record timestamps and hardware counters across each iteration. Traces capture kernel execution times on accelerator execution units, DMA transfers across PCIe or NVLink buses, and host CPU thread scheduling. Comparing elapsed time across these lanes isolates which subsystem sets the critical path. When data-loading intervals lie on the critical path and coincide with accelerator input waits, as in the GPT-2 XL profile above, the run is data-starved; speeding up matrix multiply kernels yields almost no wall-clock gain until input delivery keeps up.

Second, each diagnosed bottleneck points to a specific remedy. When the accelerator idles waiting for batches, asynchronous prefetching and double-buffering over PCIe overlap host data preparation with device compute. When kernel runtimes are bound by HBM bandwidth rather than arithmetic, kernel fusion and tiled algorithms such as FlashAttention keep intermediate activations in on-chip SRAM. When HBM capacity runs out, activation checkpointing discards and recomputes activations during the backward pass, or gradient accumulation splits each batch across sequential micro-batches to cap peak allocation. Finally, when streaming multiprocessors saturate with dense matrix math, 16-bit formats (FP16 or BF16) move eligible operations onto Tensor Cores, multiplying peak matrix throughput several-fold over FP32 CUDA cores (table 9) while halving the bytes moved per operand.

Third, combining interventions requires checking their side effects on shared hardware resources. Asynchronous prefetching hides data movement latency behind computation, but double-buffered batch queues consume pinned host memory and device HBM. Other pairs reinforce one another: combining 16-bit mixed precision with activation checkpointing cuts activation memory enough to admit a larger per-device batch size, raising the arithmetic intensity of matrix multiplications on underfed Tensor Cores. After each change, a fresh profile verifies that the secondary resource cost has not created a new bottleneck.

Figure 7 formalizes this diagnostic playbook into a triage flowchart, using low accelerator activity and high memory allocation as signals for further investigation. Idle gaps require tracing input preparation, host work, and synchronization; capacity pressure motivates memory-saving techniques. When neither signal explains a missed target, kernel profiling distinguishes bandwidth, arithmetic, and launch limits.

Each fix moves the bottleneck, so optimization is iterative and governed by Amdahl’s law: a speedup applied to one stage is capped by the share of step time that stage occupies, the bound that equation 11.1 formalizes. Resolving input starvation through asynchronous prefetching, for instance, drives accelerator utilization upward until memory bandwidth or compute saturation limits step time. Each intervention requires re-profiling to identify the newly emerged critical path, continuing the cycle until accelerator execution units operate near peak theoretical throughput or application performance targets are achieved.

8.6.2 Data prefetching and overlapping

Prefetching and overlapping put the systematic framework to work on the bottleneck the data pipeline left open. ResNet-50’s host workers prepare images more slowly than the H100 consumes them, so the accelerator is busy only about 52 percent of the time (section 8.4.2). That figure already assumes the host prepares the next batch while the accelerator computes the current one. Run serially, preparation and computation add, and the accelerator would be busy only about 34 percent. Overlap recovers the difference by hiding host work behind computation, but it cannot raise the supply rate itself, so closing the rest of the gap for ResNet-50 requires more host workers or decoding on the accelerator. The techniques below are most effective when profiling shows computational units idle while waiting for data.

In ML training, a “Read” stage is rarely a simple disk fetch. For image workloads it encompasses JPEG decoding, random crops, and color jitter applied on CPU cores before the tensor is ready to transfer; for language workloads it encompasses tokenization, subword encoding, and sequence padding. The Gantt schedule in figure 8 isolates the serial bottleneck of unbuffered ingestion. The GPU Train row remains completely idle during file-open intervals (Open 1, Open 2) and read stages (Read 1, Read 2), stretching the two-epoch schedule out to 01:35 because compute steps cannot begin until host-side decoding and transfer finish.

Framework data loaders such as PyTorch DataLoader and tf.data (Murray et al. 2021) eliminate those synchronous gaps by staging the next batch into host memory while the accelerator is still executing the current batch.

Murray, Derek G., Jiřı́ Šimša, Ana Klimovic, and Ihor Indyk. 2021. “Tf.data: A Machine Learning Data Processing Framework.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 14 (12): 2945–58. https://doi.org/10.14778/3476311.3476374.

Overlapping extends prefetching by coordinating multiple pipeline stages to execute concurrently. As the system processes the current batch on the accelerator, background host threads load and preprocess subsequent batches. Aligning the serial schedule of figure 8 against the overlapped timeline in figure 9 demonstrates this latency hiding. Concurrent staging of Open 2 and Read 3 behind active execution eliminates idle bubbles between training blocks (Train 1 through Train 3). It compresses total wall-clock duration from approximately 95 min down to 65 min (a 31.6 percent reduction). Actual gains depend on stage balance and available concurrency.

Prefetching mechanics

The overlapped schedule requires enough host workers and buffering to make the next batch ready before the device requests it. Worker count controls preparation capacity; queue depth absorbs variation in preparation time. Neither changes the slowest-stage bound established by equation 6.

Machine learning frameworks expose the control variables for this trade-off. Listing 5 demonstrates PyTorch’s DataLoader configuration, where num_workers = 4 sets preprocessing parallelism and prefetch_factor = 2 maintains a buffer of 8 batches ready for accelerator consumption.
