The parameters num_workers and prefetch_factor are not generic performance toggles. They determine CPU parallelism and buffer depth, which means they shift pressure from accelerator idle time to host memory and CPU scheduling. The third knob, pin_memory=True, lets host-to-device copies overlap accelerator execution through DMA; its cost is page-locked host memory that the rest of the system can no longer page.

↩︎ The DataLoader’s three knobs, worker count, prefetch depth, and pinned memory for DMA transfers, are established in Data loading.

Buffer management is the main trade-off. A buffer that is too small causes the GPU to wait for data preparation, reintroducing the idle time prefetching was meant to remove. An overly large host-side buffer consumes memory that could otherwise support input processing and other host work. The right configuration coordinates CPU preparation, storage I/O, and GPU computation so each resource has work ready at the moment it can use it. These techniques yield the greatest benefit when storage access is slow, preprocessing is complex, or datasets are large.

Practical considerations

Prefetch buffers and overlap depth are tunable, so the same machinery adapts to whichever stage binds, whether slow storage, limited network bandwidth, or computational throughput. Prefetching and overlapping deliver the greatest gains when preprocessing lies on the critical path but can run concurrently with model computation. In an illustrative image pipeline, random cropping (10 ms), color jittering (15 ms), and normalization (5 ms) total 30 ms per batch. Overlap can hide up to the concurrent accelerator-computation time; any excess remains on the critical path. NLP workloads similarly benefit when tokenization and subword processing would otherwise block the training loop.

The primary trade-off is memory: prefetch buffers consume host memory, or device memory when a separate device-side queue is used, in proportion to buffer depth and batch size. With batch size 256 high-resolution images (\(1024\times 1024\) pixels), one buffered batch requires approximately 3.2 GB. With num_workers = 4 and prefetch_factor = 2, the 8-batch prefetch window can hold about 25.8 GB. Tuning these parameters requires empirical testing because excessive workers contend for CPU, storage, and memory resources, while insufficient buffering reintroduces data stalls. Start with a modest worker count, increase it while measuring throughput and memory, and stop when additional workers no longer reduce accelerator idle time. When the input pipeline already exceeds compute demand, deeper prefetching adds complexity without improving throughput.

8.6.3 Mixed-precision training

Running a modern training job entirely in 32-bit floating point (FP32) leaves the accelerator’s 16-bit Tensor Cores idle and doubles the byte footprint of every forward activation kept in HBM. Mixed-precision training removes both bottlenecks by executing matrix multiplications and storing activations in a 16-bit format while retaining 32-bit precision for sensitive accumulations and parameter updates. Floating-point formats compares FP32, FP16, BF16, FP8, and INT8 precision-range trade-offs. A training recipe commonly combines FP32 with either FP16 or BF16; it does not ordinarily use FP16 and BF16 interchangeably in the same path. The resulting speed, memory, and accuracy effects depend on the workload and hardware (Micikevicius et al. 2018; Wang and Kanwar 2019; Kalamkar et al. 2019).

A neural network’s stored FP32 weights require 4 bytes per parameter, while FP16 and BF16 weights use 2 bytes. For a model with \(10^9\) parameters, the working copy of the weights shrinks from 4 GB to 2 GB. That saving does not carry over to the static training state. The common FP16 recipe keeps FP32 master weights and FP32 Adam moments. Per parameter, it spends 2 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 16 bytes on weights, gradients, master copy, and moments, exactly what FP32 Adam spends on its 4 + 4 + 8. Mixed precision’s memory benefit therefore comes from the tensors stored in 16 bits, above all the activations, together with the halved bytes moved for every 16-bit read, write, and gradient exchange.

Mixed precision’s throughput benefit is larger than its memory benefit. Figure 10 plots peak dense throughput by number format across four generations of data center accelerators. From V100 to B200 the FP32 rate grew 4.8×, the FP16/BF16 tensor rate grew 18×, and the lowest supported format, 4-bit floating point (FP4) on B200, delivers 72× the V100’s FP16 tensor rate. Hardware gains now arrive almost entirely through tensor units built for lower precision. On B200 the FP16/BF16 tensor path is 30× faster than FP32, so a training job that stays in FP32 leaves nearly all of the chip’s arithmetic idle.

The numerical differences between these formats shape their use cases. Table 9 shows that BF16’s 8-bit exponent gives it approximately the same normal exponent range as FP32, while FP16’s 5-bit exponent gives a minimum positive normal value of about \(6.1 \times 10^{-5}\) and a maximum finite value of \(65{,}504\). FP16 subnormals extend toward \(6 \times 10^{-8}\), although their treatment depends on the operation and hardware mode. FP32 provides about seven decimal digits of precision, FP16 about three, and BF16 about two to three. BF16 therefore trades mantissa precision for the exponent range that often simplifies deep-learning training.

The choice between formats depends on model characteristics and hardware support. BF16’s wider exponent range reduces overflow and underflow concerns, while FP16 provides more mantissa bits but often requires loss scaling. Framework autocast policies and fused kernels choose higher-precision accumulation for numerically sensitive operations when needed; loss reduction, softmax, normalization, and optimizer updates are common candidates, but their exact execution dtype is implementation-dependent.

Figure 11 traces this conventional FP16 execution cycle across six numbered stages. FP32 master weights are downcast to FP16 for the forward pass. Loss scaling shifts small gradient values into representable range before backpropagation, and the resulting scaled gradients are unscaled and cast back to FP32 prior to the parameter update. Modern framework implementations may store or accumulate particular tensors differently, and BF16 recipes commonly omit loss scaling because of their wider exponent range.

Modern accelerator architectures pair dense matrix execution units with reduced-precision numerical formats to maximize arithmetic throughput. NVIDIA introduced Tensor Cores for FP16 operations in the Volta architecture (NVIDIA 2017) and added native BF16 execution in Ampere (NVIDIA Corporation 2020a), matching the native BF16 support Google developed for Tensor Processing Units (Wang and Kanwar 2019). These specialized functional units execute low-precision matrix multiply-accumulate operations at four to sixteen times the rate of standard 32-bit floating-point ALUs.

NVIDIA. 2017. Training with Mixed Precision.
NVIDIA Corporation. 2020a. NVIDIA A100 Tensor Core GPU Architecture. NVIDIA Whitepaper, V1.0.

Mixed-precision training turns those hardware capabilities into a split numerical policy. Eligible matrix multiplications and convolutions execute in FP16 or BF16 on tensor units, while reductions, accumulations, and parameter updates retain higher precision. The performance gain comes from both arithmetic throughput and data movement: lower-precision units pack more multiply-accumulates into each cycle and each unit of silicon area, while narrower tensors halve data transfer volumes across caches and high-bandwidth memory. Higher precision remains necessary where wide reductions, small parameter updates, or sensitive normalizations can accumulate rounding error. Framework autocast policies enforce this split dynamically, selecting lower-precision execution for compute-intensive matrix multiplications while preserving FP32 for numerically fragile operations.

Loss scaling

FP16’s 5-bit exponent limits its representable dynamic range, creating severe underflow hazards during backpropagation. When computing gradients across deep networks, successive chain-rule multiplications frequently produce gradient magnitudes smaller than FP16’s minimum positive normal value (\(2^{-14} \approx 6.1 \times 10^{-5}\)). While IEEE 754 FP16 defines subnormal numbers down to \(\approx 6 \times 10^{-8}\), hardware accelerators often flush subnormal inputs and outputs to zero to prevent pipeline stalls. When gradients underflow to zero, weight updates stall and training fails to converge.

Loss scaling counters underflow by exploiting the linearity of differentiation: \(\nabla_{\ModelParameters} (\LossScaleFactor \cdot \LossFunction) = \LossScaleFactor \cdot \nabla_{\ModelParameters} \LossFunction\). Multiplying the scalar loss \(\LossFunction\) by a large scaling factor \(\LossScaleFactor\) (such as \(2^{10}\) or \(2^{15}\)) prior to backpropagation shifts the gradient distribution rightward into FP16’s normal representable range. Once backpropagation completes, the accumulated gradients are divided by \(\LossScaleFactor\) (unscaled) before applying the optimizer update.

Deep learning frameworks automate this process through dynamic loss scaling; Mixed-precision training support describes the GradScaler machinery that scales the loss, unscales the gradients, and skips any step whose gradients overflow. The training decision is how the scale factor adapts. Because gradient norms fluctuate during training, a static scale factor risks either underflow (if \(\LossScaleFactor\) is too small) or numerical overflow past FP16’s maximum finite value of \(65{,}504\) (if \(\LossScaleFactor\) is too large). A dynamic scaler therefore skips each overflowing step so that nonfinite gradients never reach the FP32 master weights, halves \(\LossScaleFactor\) at each skip, and doubles \(\LossScaleFactor\) after a configurable growth window without overflow (such as 2,000 consecutive steps). Together these adjustments keep gradients near the upper end of the dynamic range without saturating.

Mixed-precision benefits

Once dynamic loss scaling or BF16 preserves gradient fidelity, mixed-precision training compounds gains across memory footprint, arithmetic throughput, and communication volume. Although the 50 percent reduction in working weight storage (from 4 GB to 2 GB on a one-billion-parameter model) is absorbed by the 16-bytes-per-parameter static master weights and Adam moments derived above, forward activations shrink by the full 50 percent. Computationally, reduced precision routes dense matrix multiplication kernels to high-throughput Tensor Core pipelines (section 8.6.3.3), while in distributed topologies, transferring 16-bit rather than 32-bit gradients halves collective AllReduce traffic across the interconnect. Because reclaimed activation memory permits larger micro-batches, Tensor Cores run at higher arithmetic intensity while narrower tensors reduce gradient exchange latency. The following case study evaluates these memory effects on GPT-2 XL at batch 32, the configuration the activation notebook left far over the H100’s capacity.

Napkin Math 8.6: GPT-2 mixed precision memory savings
Problem: What does mixed precision save when GPT-2 XL (1.5B parameters) trains at batch 32 and sequence length 1,024 on one H100, and what does it leave unchanged?

FP32 baseline:

  • Parameters and gradients (FP32): 12 GB (6 GB parameters + 6 GB gradients)
  • Activations: ~574 GB
  • Optimizer states (Adam first and second moments in FP32): 12 GB
  • Total: ~598 GB

FP16 mixed precision:

  • Parameters (FP16): 1.5B parameters at 2 bytes each = 3 GB
  • Activations (FP16): ~287 GB
  • Gradients (FP16): 3 GB
  • FP32 master weights: 6 GB (for precise optimizer updates)
  • Optimizer states (Adam first and second moments in FP32): 12 GB
  • Total: ~311 GB, about 3.6× the H100’s 80 GB

Systems insight: Mixed precision halves the activations and leaves the static state unchanged at 24 GB. At batch 32 the remaining activations still dominate, and most of them are the quadratic attention-score matrices, which FlashAttention (section 8.6.4) removes next.

Memory savings alone do not guarantee stable training. In the FP16 training recipe used here, maintaining FP32 master weights is necessary because parameter updates \(\FiniteChange \ModelWeight= -\LearningRate \cdot \LossGradient\) (learning rate times gradient) are typically orders of magnitude smaller than the weight values \(\ModelWeight\). In FP16, where the 10-bit mantissa provides roughly three decimal digits of precision, adding an update that is more than \(2^{11} \approx 2048\) times smaller than the weight magnitude results in arithmetic swamping: the updated weight rounds back to its original value. This loss of an increment through rounding is distinct from the update value itself underflowing. Retaining master weights in FP32 preserves these incremental updates across thousands of optimizer steps, casting to 16 bits only when staging weights for the forward pass. Autocast policies similarly isolate wide reductions, layer normalizations, and softmax layers in FP32 to prevent catastrophic cancellation.

Once numerical stability is established, reduced-precision arithmetic and reduced memory traffic can shorten execution when they reduce work on the critical path. Comparing complete training recipes does not isolate the contribution of precision alone; model quality, batch size, optimizer representation, and activation policy must be accounted for. The following scenario models these compound throughput, schedule, and infrastructure cost gains for a GPT-2 XL training run.

Napkin Math 8.7: GPT-2 mixed precision throughput and cost
Problem: Holding utilization fixed, how much faster and cheaper is GPT-2 XL’s run on one H100 when its matrix products run in BF16 instead of in 32-bit formats?

Peak rates by path: FP32 on scalar units 67 TFLOP/s; TF32 on Tensor Cores 494.5 TFLOP/s; BF16 on Tensor Cores 989 TFLOP/s.

Run time and cost at 50 percent utilization and $4/hour per accelerator:

  • FP32: about 518 days, $49,751
  • TF32: about 70 days, $6,741
  • BF16: about 35 days, $3,370

Quality gate: The comparison is valid only if a controlled validation run shows that the BF16 recipe meets the same acceptance criteria as the 32-bit baseline. The scenario does not assume a particular perplexity difference.

Systems insight: The peak ratios, 2× over TF32 and 15× over FP32, bound the gain rather than predict it. Holding utilization fixed assumes that every part of the step speeds up with the matrix units, but elementwise kernels, optimizer updates, and input waits do not run on Tensor Cores, so by Amdahl’s law the measured speedup is smaller. A real decision requires measured throughput, billing, and quality validation for the target workload.

Realizing these speedups requires empirical profiling rather than assuming automatic acceleration. For small models or memory-bound workloads not dominated by large matrix multiplications, data type casting and kernel launch overheads can offset the arithmetic throughput advantage. Furthermore, whether an accelerator supports native BF16 or requires FP16 loss scaling depends on the underlying microarchitecture.

Mixed-precision hardware support

Specialized matrix execution units deliver the wall-clock speedup of mixed precision by spending fewer bits per operand. Accelerators pair these low-precision multiply units with higher-precision accumulation registers to maintain numerical stability during dense matrix reductions.

The Volta-generation Tensor Cores introduced earlier in this section perform \(4{\times}4\) matrix multiply-accumulate operations in a single clock cycle, a datapath that Tensor Cores examines in detail. For FP16 inputs, a single Tensor Core executes: \[ \TensorCoreOutputMatrix= \TensorCoreLeftInput\times \TensorCoreRightInput+ \TensorCoreAccumulatorMatrix\] where \(\TensorCoreLeftInput\) and \(\TensorCoreRightInput\) are FP16 inputs and \(\TensorCoreAccumulatorMatrix\) and \(\TensorCoreOutputMatrix\) may use FP32 accumulation. Higher-precision accumulation reduces rounding error, although it does not eliminate numerical error or cancellation.

Hardware peak specifications define the theoretical ceiling on this acceleration. The reference H100 exposes 67 TFLOP/s of FP32 throughput on standard CUDA cores and 989 TFLOP/s of dense FP16 or BF16 Tensor Core throughput, a 15× peak ratio. It adds FP8 Tensor Cores at 1,979 TFLOP/s without sparsity, about 2× its FP16/BF16 rate. These figures represent hardware ceilings rather than end-to-end training guarantees: matrix multiplications map directly to Tensor Cores, but data movement, non-Tensor-Core kernels, communication, loss scaling, and optimizer updates remain. While the batched matrix multiplication \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) in transformer self-attention (\(2 \times \BatchSize\times \AttentionHeadCount \times \SequenceLength^2 \times \AttentionHeadDimension\) FLOPs) maps directly to these tensor units, Amdahl’s law dictates that the remaining memory-bound elementwise operations, communication collectives, and optimizer updates bound the realized end-to-end speedup.

Microarchitecture generation also determines whether a pipeline must run the dynamic loss-scaling loop of section 8.6.3.1. Volta and Turing Tensor Cores support only FP16, where gradients below \(6.1 \times 10^{-5}\) risk subnormal flushing to zero unless scaled. On Google TPUs and on NVIDIA GPUs from Ampere (A100) onward, native Brain Float 16 (BF16) Tensor Core paths preserve FP32’s 8-bit exponent range (down to \(\approx 10^{-38}\) at 7 mantissa bits), avoiding loss scaling while still relying on FP32 accumulation registers to limit rounding error.

NVIDIA’s Hopper architecture (H100) adds FP8 Tensor Core paths for 8-bit floating-point values (1.98 PFLOP/s on H100 dense vs. 0.99 PFLOP/s for FP16 dense, without sparsity); FP8’s two bit layouts and the per-tensor scaling they require are developed in Numerical formats. Table 10 summarizes when each precision is appropriate:

In addition to accelerating compute-bound matrix operations, reduced precision relieves memory bandwidth bottlenecks by altering operational arithmetic intensity. On modern accelerators, large matrix multiplications reside in the compute-bound regime of the roofline model, whereas elementwise operations (such as bias additions, layer normalization, and residual connections) reside firmly in the memory-bound regime. The H100 illustrates the physical imbalance between arithmetic capability and memory bandwidth: its HBM3 bus supplies 3,350 GB/s, whereas sustaining its 67 TFLOP/s of FP32 throughput without operand reuse would demand 268 TB/s if every FLOP required fresh data from memory. Although operand reuse reduces actual traffic for dense matrix multiplications, operations with low arithmetic intensity remain strictly bandwidth-limited.

For memory-bound kernels, execution time is determined by bytes transferred across the memory bus rather than arithmetic clock cycles. Converting 32-bit operands to 16-bit FP16 or BF16 halves the byte volume transferred across the HBM bus, doubling the theoretical throughput of memory-bound operations even without engaging Tensor Cores. Realized bandwidth gains depend on cache hit rates, kernel fusion, and format conversion costs.

Mixed-precision training support describes how autocast and GradScaler implement the split between 16-bit and FP32 operations inside the framework. The policy decision that remains for the training recipe is which format to run, and listing 6 makes it a single switch: the FP16 path enables gradient scaling, and the BF16 path disables it.

Because hardware support for low-precision formats evolved across accelerator generations, the optimal mixed-precision recipe depends on the target architecture. Table 11 outlines the precision trade-offs across three generations of NVIDIA GPUs.

Throughput gains across these generations came from new tensor execution units built for narrower formats, and measured speedups also reflect software stacks, kernels, and batch dimensions that changed alongside the hardware. The peak-rate ratios in Notebook 8.7 bound what a format switch can deliver on one H100. In practice, BF16 is the default where the hardware supports it, and FP16 with loss scaling is the fallback.

8.6.4 FlashAttention: IO-aware attention optimization

Mixed-precision training addresses two bottlenecks, compute throughput because Tensor Cores run faster on 16-bit operands, and memory capacity because each value uses fewer bytes. For transformer models, a third bottleneck often dominates, and the roofline analysis has already located it. Counting only its score-matrix traffic, materialized attention in GPT-2 Small reaches about 32 FLOP/byte, well left of the H100 ridge point in figure 1, because its quadratic intermediate matrices are written to HBM during the forward pass and read back during backpropagation. Halving the bytes per value does not change that quadratic traffic, so even with reduced precision the compute units can sit idle while the processor waits for data.

The same quadratic term dominates GPT-2 XL’s memory. At batch 32 and 1,024 tokens, the stored score, probability, and dropout matrices account for about 201 GB of the 287 GB of mixed-precision activations, about 70 percent. An attention kernel that never stores them leaves 85.6 GB of activations; with the static state the step needs 109.6 GB, still about 24 GB more than the H100’s 80 GB (85.9 GB in decimal units). Removing attention’s traffic is therefore necessary but not sufficient.

FlashAttention (Dao et al. 2022) addresses this bandwidth bottleneck by treating attention as an IO problem. Processing attention in SRAM-sized tiles avoids materializing the full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) matrix in HBM and reduces traffic without changing the mathematical output, which is how the roofline preview moved attention rightward toward, and for favorable shapes past, the ridge.

The standard attention memory bottleneck

Standard self-attention materializes an \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) score matrix and an \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) probability matrix in off-chip HBM for every attention head, turning long-sequence training into a memory-bandwidth and capacity bottleneck:

\[ \text{Attention}(\AttentionQueryMatrix, \AttentionKeyMatrix, \AttentionValuesMatrix) = \text{softmax}\left(\frac{\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T}{\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}}\right)\AttentionValuesMatrix \tag{9}\]

Here, \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) are the query, key, and value matrices for a sequence of length \(\SequenceLength\), and \(\AttentionHeadDimension\) is the key-vector dimension used to scale the dot products before softmax. Because the intermediate product \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) is written out to HBM before softmax and read back immediately afterward, attention becomes an I/O problem even when the matrix products themselves map cleanly onto Tensor Cores.

The memory bottleneck emerges from materializing the \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) intermediate matrices for scores and probabilities. For a sequence length of 4,096 with head dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension =\) 64 (typical for a single attention head), the attention score matrix alone requires \(4{,}096^2 \times 4\) bytes = 67.1 MB in FP32. This section counts FP32 scores throughout, since autocast policies commonly keep softmax in FP32; at FP16 every figure halves, as in the roofline estimate, while the quadratic growth stays the same. With 16 heads, this grows to 1.1 GB for the intermediate attention matrices alone, not including the keys, queries, values, or output tensors.

GPU memory hierarchy creates the opportunity for I/O-aware attention. HBM offers large off-chip capacity, while much smaller on-chip SRAM has substantially higher bandwidth and lower latency. Attention implementations that materialize the quadratic intermediates in HBM must write and later read them during backpropagation. Because that traffic grows with the square of sequence length, it takes a growing share of step time as sequences lengthen.

The backward pass requires the information represented by the attention probabilities, but an implementation may either save those intermediates or recompute them. For \(\AttentionWeightMatrix = \text{softmax}(\AttentionScoreMatrix)\) and \(\AttentionScoreMatrix = \AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\), one gradient expression is: \[ \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \AttentionQueryMatrix} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}} \cdot \text{dsoftmax}\!\left(\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \AttentionWeightMatrix},\, \AttentionWeightMatrix\right) \cdot \AttentionKeyMatrix\] where \(\text{dsoftmax}\) denotes the softmax Jacobian-vector product. A conventional implementation may save the probability matrix for this computation; saving both probabilities and raw scores is not mathematically required. FlashAttention instead saves compact normalization statistics and recomputes tiled score and probability blocks during backward propagation, avoiding full quadratic intermediates in HBM.

IO-aware attention through tiling

FlashAttention eliminates the need to materialize full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention matrices in HBM by computing attention incrementally through tiling. Instead of computing the entire attention matrix at once, the algorithm partitions \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) into tiles small enough to fit in fast SRAM, computes attention scores for these tiles, and incrementally accumulates results.

The key algorithmic insight relies on the structure of equation 9. Softmax normalizes each row of scores by a sum over the entire row, which seems to require the whole row before any output can be formed, yet the online softmax algorithm removes that requirement. Ordinary softmax, \(\text{softmax}(\SoftmaxInputVector)_i = e^{\SoftmaxInputVector_i} / \sum_j e^{\SoftmaxInputVector_j}\), is computed stably by first subtracting the row maximum. The online form instead keeps two running statistics per row as blocks of scores arrive: the largest score seen so far, \(\SoftmaxRunningMaximum\), and the sum of exponentials taken relative to that maximum, \(\SoftmaxNormalizer\). When a new block raises the maximum from \(\SoftmaxRunningMaximum\) to \(\UpdatedSoftmaxRunningMaximum\), everything accumulated so far was exponentiated relative to the old maximum, and multiplying it by \(e^{\SoftmaxRunningMaximum-\UpdatedSoftmaxRunningMaximum}\) re-expresses it relative to the new one. The same factor rescales the partially accumulated output. After the last block, dividing the output by \(\SoftmaxNormalizer\) gives exactly the softmax-weighted result, as if the whole row had been seen at once.

Algorithm 2 decomposes this computation by partitioning the queries and the keys/values into tiles small enough to fit in SRAM, then streaming over the key/value tiles while maintaining the running softmax statistics needed to assemble each output tile, without ever forming the full score matrix.

FlashAttention swaps the full attention matrix for small SRAM tiles.

No full \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) score or probability matrix is written to HBM. A \(128{\times}128\) FP32 score tile, for example, occupies 65.5 KB compared with 67.1 MB for a \(4096{\times}4096\) matrix. Other inputs, outputs, and running statistics remain, so the tile is not necessarily the largest tensor in the entire kernel.

The storage boundary distinguishes the tiled algorithm from the materialized implementation. Figure 12 follows the same inputs and output through both paths; only the materialized path writes the full intermediate matrices to HBM.

Keeping the intermediate tiles on chip removes their HBM writeback and reload. It does not remove the input reads or output writes, and backward propagation still requires saved statistics and recomputation.

\(\AttentionQueryMatrix\)

Memory and IO complexity analysis

FlashAttention improves both activation memory and memory IO, which are the limiting costs in bandwidth-bound attention. Standard attention requires \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) memory to store score matrices \(\AttentionScoreMatrix\) and attention-probability matrices \(\AttentionWeightMatrix\) across all sequence positions. FlashAttention reduces the stored activation footprint to \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) by keeping only input and output tensors \((\AttentionQueryMatrix, \AttentionKeyMatrix, \AttentionValuesMatrix, \AttentionHeadOutput)\) plus a small SRAM buffer for the current tile.

For \(\SequenceLength = 4096\), \(\AttentionHeadDimension = 64\), one materialized FP32 score matrix occupies \(4096^2 \times 4\) bytes = 67.1 MB per head. The three FP32 input tensors occupy \((3 \times 4096{\times}64) \times 4\) bytes ≈ 3.1 MB per head, a component-size ratio of 21.3× between one quadratic intermediate and the inputs. The comparison that decides memory is the whole per-head footprint each algorithm keeps for the backward pass. The conventional baseline assumed here stores the inputs, the output, and both the score and probability matrices, about 138.4 MB. FlashAttention stores the inputs, the output, and two numbers per row (the running maximum and sum), about 4.2 MB, roughly 32.7× smaller, and the gap widens as \(\SequenceLength\) grows because only the standard footprint is quadratic.

These stored-tensor totals exclude gradient buffers, temporary workspace, and the live SRAM tile, so they are not estimates of peak training memory. Implementations may also retain fewer quadratic intermediates than the conventional baseline assumed here.

The IO pattern changes for the same reason. Standard attention reads \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) from HBM, writes score, probability, and output matrices during the forward pass, then rereads those stored matrices during the backward pass before writing the gradients \(d\AttentionQueryMatrix\), \(d\AttentionKeyMatrix\), and \(d\AttentionValuesMatrix\) (shorthand for \(\partial \LossFunction/\partial \AttentionQueryMatrix\) and its counterparts). The resulting HBM traffic includes the same \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) term that made the activation footprint large. FlashAttention forms score and probability blocks in SRAM and never writes the full \(\SequenceLength \times \SequenceLength\) tensors to HBM. In the backward pass, it recomputes the needed blocks rather than reading stored full matrices. Because each outer-loop pass holds a query tile in on-chip SRAM while streaming the key and value tiles past it from HBM, an entire row of score blocks is computed on chip for one HBM read of the keys and values. Larger SRAM holds larger tiles and requires fewer streaming passes over HBM, so the exact HBM IO bound scales inversely with available SRAM capacity rather than dropping all the way to \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength \cdot \HiddenDimension)\).

Both approaches require \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2 \HiddenDimension)\) asymptotic FLOPs for dense attention, so FlashAttention’s gain comes from traffic, not arithmetic. The resulting reduction in HBM traffic can shift the kernel toward compute-bound execution and produce a net speedup, but the final bottleneck remains shape- and hardware-dependent.

Implementation and hardware utilization

FlashAttention’s performance gains materialize through careful exploitation of GPU memory hierarchy. Modern frameworks can dispatch eligible attention operations among fused and fallback implementations based on hardware, inputs, and layouts; profiling verifies which path ran and whether it helped. Listing 7 contrasts standard and optimized attention implementations.

Dao et al. (2022) reports end-to-end GPT-style training speedups and separate attention-kernel benchmarks showing that IO-aware attention reduces memory traffic and improves runtime. Runtime and memory savings depend on batch size, head count, sequence length, precision, and the kernel implementation, and a capacity threshold still requires the complete training memory budget, including the state outside attention. Later versions refine the same idea: FlashAttention-2 (Dao 2023) reports a further 1.5–2\(\times\) speedup through better parallelism, and FlashAttention-3 (Shah et al. 2024) adds asynchronous memory operations and FP8 support on Hopper GPUs.

Dao, T., D. Y. Fu, S. Ermon, A. Rudra, and C. Ré. 2022. “FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 16344–59. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1189.
Dao, Tri. 2023. “FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.08691.
Shah, Jay, Ganesh Bikshandi, Ying Zhang, Vijay Thakkar, Pradeep Ramani, and Tri Dao. 2024. “FlashAttention-3: Fast and Accurate Attention with Asynchrony and Low-Precision.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 37 (NeurIPS), 68658–85. https://doi.org/10.52202/079017-2193.

When to use FlashAttention

For transformer training with long sequences, FlashAttention is usually the first attention implementation to test. It often becomes essential once sequence length pushes standard attention into an HBM-capacity or HBM-bandwidth bottleneck, especially around the multi-thousand-token regime where the \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention matrices dominate memory. A100- and H100-class GPUs with fast on-chip SRAM benefit most. The returns diminish for short sequences where tiling overhead is not worthwhile, on older GPU architectures without comparable SRAM bandwidth, and for nonattention architectures such as convolutional neural networks and multilayer perceptrons.

In practice, deep learning frameworks handle much of FlashAttention’s integration, but the decision still depends on layout, precision, and memory budget. PyTorch’s scaled_dot_product_attention can dispatch to FlashAttention when hardware, inputs, and backend constraints permit; hand-written attention must use an eligible primitive. Three practical checks determine whether the fast path actually runs and pays off. Tensor layouts must match library expectations (contiguous memory and the expected dimension ordering). Execution must use FP16 or BF16, because FlashAttention kernels target mixed precision. Gradient checkpointing should be combined with FlashAttention whenever additional activation-memory savings are needed beyond the attention scores.

The integration is often a single-line change, swapping a manual attention call for F.scaled_dot_product_attention, as listing 7 shows. The engineering decision is still the same one established by the roofline analysis: use the optimized primitive when the bottleneck is HBM traffic, and let the library manage the tiling and SRAM scheduling needed to remove it.

IO-aware design beyond attention

FlashAttention is an instance of IO-aware algorithm design: because modern accelerators are compute-abundant relative to memory bandwidth, traffic governs runtime for a bandwidth-bound kernel even when FLOPs do not change. This principle extends beyond attention. In IO-aware matrix multiplication, tiling algorithms like those in CUTLASS minimize DRAM traffic by maximizing data reuse in fast caches. A naive \(\SquareGemmDimension{\times}\SquareGemmDimension\) matrix multiply performs \(\mathcal{O}(\SquareGemmDimension^3)\) FLOPs and, without reuse, \(\mathcal{O}(\SquareGemmDimension^3)\) memory traffic; blocking keeps tiles in fast memory and moves traffic toward the \(\mathcal{O}(\SquareGemmDimension^2)\) lower bound.

The same byte-movement principle reappears in communication-efficient distributed training, where gradient compression trades extra computation (compression/decompression) for reduced network bandwidth consumption. Low-power edge devices with limited memory bandwidth benefit even more from IO-aware algorithms. Trading 10 percent more arithmetic to halve memory traffic approaches a 2\(\times\) energy reduction in the limit where data movement accounts for the entire budget. It falls short of that bound whenever arithmetic carries a meaningful share, because halving one additive term of a sum cannot more than halve the sum.

Eliminating off-chip materialization of the \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) attention matrices reclaims accelerator memory across three architectural trade-offs. First, it enables longer sequence contexts on fixed hardware, with the workable context boundary determined by the complete training recipe. Second, freeing activation memory allows larger micro-batch sizes that improve hardware arithmetic saturation, provided gradient dynamics remain stable. Third, the contracted activation footprint permits deeper transformer stacks within a given memory budget, although end-to-end multi-billion-parameter feasibility continues to depend on parameter sharding, optimizer states, and remaining layer activations.

FlashAttention performs the same arithmetic but moves fewer bytes, which is why counting bytes before FLOPs is the first step on any bandwidth-bound kernel. Bandwidth is not the only memory limit, however. The capacity required to hold activations and optimizer states at the same time can still exceed the accelerator, and when models or batch sizes outgrow device memory, two complementary techniques trade computation for memory.

8.6.5 Gradient accumulation and checkpointing

FlashAttention left GPT-2 XL’s batch-32 step about 24 GB over the H100’s capacity, and the excess now consists of ordinary activations, the layer inputs, projections, and feed-forward intermediates that backpropagation must read. Two techniques remove it without adding hardware. Gradient accumulation builds the batch from smaller micro-batches that each fit, and activation checkpointing keeps fewer activations and recomputes the rest during the backward pass. Both were named earlier, accumulation with mini-batch processing (section 8.3.2.1.2) and recomputation with activation memory (section 8.3.3.2). Using them correctly requires knowing when accumulated gradients equal the large-batch gradient, what each technique costs in time, how checkpoints are placed (the \(\sqrt{\LayerCount}\) schedule), and which loss-normalization errors most often break accumulation.

Gradient accumulation and checkpointing mechanics

Gradient accumulation changes how many examples contribute to one update; checkpointing changes which activations remain resident between the forward and backward passes.

Gradient accumulation

To execute large batch training on memory-constrained accelerators, systems split an effective logical batch across smaller sequential micro-batches. Three consecutive micro-batches evaluate independent losses (\(\LossFunction_1, \LossFunction_2, \LossFunction_3\)) and gradients (\(\MicrobatchGradient_1, \MicrobatchGradient_2, \MicrobatchGradient_3\), where \(\MicrobatchGradient_j = \nabla_{\ModelParameters}\LossFunction_j\)), accumulating into a single unified tensor (\(\MicrobatchGradient_1 + \MicrobatchGradient_2 + \MicrobatchGradient_3\)) before triggering the optimizer step (figure 13).

In PyTorch, gradient accumulation is usually implemented by dividing each micro-batch loss by the number of accumulation steps and calling optimizer.step() only after processing the entire effective batch. Under that averaging convention, no learning-rate adjustment is needed solely because of accumulation. The implementation follows five steps:

  1. Perform the forward pass for a micro-batch.
  2. Compute the gradients during the backward pass.
  3. Accumulate the gradients into a buffer without updating the model parameters.
  4. Repeat steps 1–3 for all micro-batches in the effective batch.
  5. Update the model parameters using the accumulated gradients after all micro-batches are processed.

Gradient accumulation can reproduce the gradient of a larger batch under controlled assumptions. For an effective batch size \(\BatchSize= \AccumulationStepCount\times \MicrobatchSize\) where \(\AccumulationStepCount\) is the number of accumulation steps and \(\MicrobatchSize\) is the micro-batch size, equation 10 confirms that the accumulated gradient equals the true batch gradient: \[ \nabla \FullBatchLoss = \frac{1}{\BatchSize}\sum_{i=1}^{\BatchSize} \nabla \LossFunction_i = \frac{1}{\AccumulationStepCount}\sum_{j=1}^{\AccumulationStepCount}\left(\frac{1}{\MicrobatchSize}\sum_{i \in \MiniBatchSet_j} \nabla \LossFunction_i\right) \tag{10}\]

This equivalence holds when each example’s computation is identical and the loss is averaged consistently. The right-hand side shows that averaging \(\AccumulationStepCount\) micro-batch gradients (each computed over \(\MicrobatchSize\) examples) produces the same result as computing the gradient over all \(\BatchSize= \AccumulationStepCount \MicrobatchSize\) examples at once. Stateful layers such as BatchNorm, stochastic operations such as dropout and data augmentation, finite-precision accumulation order, and step-indexed schedules can break exact equivalence, so large-batch accumulation still requires validation.

Gradient accumulation exchanges memory capacity for computation time. Table 12 separates the memory benefit from the compute and scheduling costs that remain.

Accumulation preserves the total example-level arithmetic but can change efficiency because smaller micro-batches use the accelerator differently and launch kernels more often. With distributed no_sync() accumulation, one gradient synchronization occurs after the \(\AccumulationStepCount\) local micro-batches rather than after each one. The model in equation 11 gives the per-update time: \[ \AccumulatedUpdateTime= \sum_{j=1}^{\AccumulationStepCount} \MicrobatchPassTime+ \AccumulatedSyncTime+ \OptimizerStepTime \tag{11}\]

Here \(\MicrobatchPassTime\) is the measured forward/backward time for micro-batch \(\MicrobatchIndex\), \(\AccumulatedSyncTime\) is the synchronization time paid for the accumulated update, and \(\OptimizerStepTime\) is the optimizer-step time. In distributed data-parallel training, standard framework wrappers automatically fire asynchronous gradient-synchronization hooks during every backward pass. When accumulating gradients across \(\AccumulationStepCount\) micro-batches, leaving these hooks active would trigger redundant AllReduce operations on every micro-batch; wrapping the first \(\AccumulationStepCount-1\) micro-batches in a model.no_sync() context suppresses this communication, synchronizing gradients only on the final \(\AccumulationStepCount\)-th step. That synchronization boundary may contain several bucketed AllReduce calls.

The wall-clock penalty relative to processing the effective batch at once is workload-dependent and must be measured rather than inferred from the memory-reduction factor. When gradient accumulation is combined with distributed data parallelism across multiple machines, additional considerations arise for gradient synchronization timing and effective batch size calculation across the cluster; advanced distributed systems texts treat these patterns in depth.

Activation checkpointing

Activation checkpointing reduces activation memory by trading additional FLOPs for lower HBM occupancy during backpropagation. Only selected boundary tensors are retained in device memory; intermediate activations are dropped during the forward pass and recomputed on demand as the backward pass traverses in reverse (figure 14, where retained tensors are solid green, dropped ones dashed gray, and recomputed ones orange). Algorithm 3 states the schedule layer by layer.

\(\NumberOfLayers\)

In this segment schedule, recomputed intermediate values remain available until their backward consumers finish; recomputing a segment afresh for each layer would add more work. For layers with comparable activation sizes and forward costs, spacing checkpoints uniformly every \(\sqrt{\LayerCount}\) layers reduces peak activation memory from \(\mathcal{O}(\LayerCount)\) to \(\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\LayerCount})\) at the cost of one additional forward pass of arithmetic per training step (Chen et al. 2016). This increases executed step computation from roughly \(3\times\) to \(4\times\) the forward-pass FLOPs, trading extra arithmetic operations for a substantially reduced activation footprint. The useful model FLOP count \(\Operations\) is unchanged, so the additional work enters the training-time law through effective utilization \(\EfficiencyHw\). Actual fit and wall-clock overhead still depend on the full memory budget and execution profile. Section 8.6.5.2 derives why \(\sqrt{\LayerCount}\) spacing minimizes this footprint and works out its cost for GPT-2’s 48 layers.

Frameworks like PyTorch provide tools such as torch.utils.checkpoint that split a model into segments and manage the recomputation, but the recompute cost remains. Checkpointing is most effective for deep architectures with dozens or hundreds of layers, such as transformers or large convolutional networks, where activation storage exceeds the GPU’s capacity before arithmetic throughput is exhausted.

The two techniques address different terms. Gradient accumulation bounds the activation memory tied to batch size, while checkpointing bounds the memory held by each layer’s intermediate activations, so together they widen the range of models that fit on available hardware.

Optimal checkpoint placement strategy

Algorithm 3 stated the \(\sqrt{\LayerCount}\) schedule without justifying it. For a network with \(\LayerCount\) layers of comparable activation size and recomputation cost, each storing \(\ActivationMemory^{(\LayerIndex)}\) bytes of activations, table 13 quantifies how the number and placement of checkpoints determines the memory-compute trade-off. Sub-linear checkpointing strategies can reduce memory consumption from \(\mathcal{O}(\LayerCount)\) to \(\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\LayerCount})\) with additional forward arithmetic; the resulting wall-clock overhead depends on execution, while the reduced footprint can enable deeper models on existing hardware.

The memory column counts two things, the stored checkpoints and the one segment that must be rematerialized at a time during backward, which holds \(\LayerCount/\ActivationCheckpointCount\) layers of activations. The one-checkpoint row shows why both terms matter. With a single checkpoint the only segment spans the whole network, so backward rebuilds all \(\LayerCount\) layers of activations at once and the peak is no lower than storing everything. Checkpoints pay off only when they are spread out so that each recomputed segment is short. The recompute column counts layer forwards. Every layer that is not itself a checkpoint is run forward once more, \((\LayerCount - \ActivationCheckpointCount)\) layer forwards in all, which is nearly one full forward pass when \(\ActivationCheckpointCount\) is much smaller than \(\LayerCount\).

Setting the derivative of total memory cost \((\ActivationCheckpointCount\times \ActivationMemory^{(\ell)} + (\LayerCount/\ActivationCheckpointCount) \times \ActivationMemory^{(\ell)})\) with respect to the checkpoint count \(\ActivationCheckpointCount\) to zero yields the analytical optimum at \(\ActivationCheckpointCount= \sqrt{\LayerCount}\). In the standard \(\sqrt{\LayerCount}\) checkpointing scheme, this costs roughly one extra forward pass, about one-third more total step compute, in exchange for minimizing activation footprint under this analytical model. For GPT-2 with forty-eight transformer layers, the operational difference is substantial. Uncheckpointed training requires \(48 \times \ActivationMemory^{(\ell)}\) in full activation storage. Uniform checkpointing (\(\sqrt{48} \approx 7\) checkpoints) requires \(7 \times \ActivationMemory^{(\ell)} + (48/7) \times \ActivationMemory^{(\ell)} \approx 14 \times \ActivationMemory^{(\ell)}\), which saves 71 percent of activation memory for approximately 33 percent additional total step computation (by recomputing one forward pass over the uncheckpointed layers during backpropagation).

The GPT-2 notebook below uses eight equal segments of six layers. It applies the same boundary-plus-live-segment accounting with unequal storage costs: a segment input contains only the hidden tensor, whereas the reconstructed layer state also includes attention and feed-forward intermediates. The equal-state optimum above is therefore a useful baseline, not an optimality claim for that transformer schedule. Counting the two components separately produces the candidate memory budget without assuming a reduction factor.

Napkin Math 8.8: GPT-2 segment checkpointing
Problem: With mixed precision and FlashAttention-style attention, can a segment schedule fit GPT-2 XL’s batch-32 step on one H100, and what does it cost in arithmetic?

Schedule: Partition the 48 transformer layers into 8 contiguous segments of 6 layers. During forward execution, retain each segment’s input and discard its internal backward state. During backward execution, reconstruct one segment, retain its internal state while differentiating that segment, then free it before reconstructing the preceding segment. Each layer therefore executes one additional forward pass; against a forward-plus-backward step costing about three forward passes, the extra arithmetic is about one-third. This is work accounting, not a wall-clock penalty.

Math: Each retained segment input has shape batch \(\times\) sequence length \(\times\) hidden width, 32 \(\times\) 1,024 \(\times\) 1600 \(\times\) 2 bytes. Keep all 8 inputs resident while adding the complete saved-state estimate for the 6 layers of one reconstructed segment. This deliberately counts the active segment’s input in both terms, making the estimate conservative rather than omitting a live boundary.

  • Retained segment inputs: 0.84 GB
  • Live reconstructed segment: 10.7 GB
  • Activation allowance: 11.5 GB, against 85.6 GB without checkpointing (7.4× smaller)
  • Static state plus activations: 35.5 GB, leaving about 50 GB of the H100’s capacity

Systems insight: Checkpointing admits the batch-32 step by replacing retained layer interiors with small boundary tensors and one live reconstructed segment. The remaining capacity must still hold output and loss buffers, temporary backward workspaces, and allocator overhead, which a runtime peak-memory measurement confirms. Gradient accumulation offers the alternative trade, keeping every activation of a smaller micro-batch and paying in update frequency instead of recomputation.

Not all operations are equally expensive to recompute, which motivates selective checkpointing: the practical rule is to spend recomputation on high-memory layers rather than on operations whose activations are already cheap to retain. Table 14 compares where activation memory is large enough to justify recomputation.

Memory and computational benefits

Both techniques reduce activation-memory requirements without adding hardware, though each targets a different dimension of the activation footprint. Gradient accumulation produces an effective batch larger than the resident micro-batch without storing all of its activations simultaneously. A larger effective batch reduces sampling variance, but it does not by itself guarantee faster convergence or better generalization. Conversely, activation checkpointing reduces the per-layer memory footprint of intermediate activations by discarding and recomputing them as needed, freeing capacity for deeper models, additional layers, or higher-resolution inputs. For lighthouse 8.1, accumulation provides a direct way to reach a chosen effective batch size while keeping the resident micro-batch within one H100’s memory.

Napkin Math 8.9: GPT-2 gradient accumulation strategy
This GPT-2-scale scenario uses the lighthouse’s one-accelerator run time and a registry rental price to show gradient accumulation; it does not reconstruct GPT-2’s historical training bill.

Memory Constraints

  • With FlashAttention-style attention and no checkpointing, a micro-batch of \(\MicrobatchSize=\) 16 needs about 67 GB, within one H100’s 80 GB.
  • A resident effective batch of \(\BatchSize=\) 512 would therefore require 512 ÷ 16 = 32 accelerators.

Accumulation Configuration

  • Use 8 accelerators and accumulate 4 micro-batches of 16 sequences each.
  • Each accelerator processes 16 \(\times\) 4 = 64 sequences per update, giving a global effective batch of 8 accelerators \(\times\) 64 = 512.

A distributed no_sync() context can defer AllReduce until the final micro-batch.

Trade-off Analysis

  • Idealized wall-clock time: 4× longer (105 hours against 26 hours) because fewer accelerators perform the same arithmetic work
  • Memory overhead: accumulation normally reuses parameter-gradient buffers, although bookkeeping and temporary storage remain
  • Communication: both configurations synchronize once per 512-sequence update, but participant count and topology differ
  • Equal-work cost: $3,370 either way at $4/hour per accelerator, before utilization and communication effects

Why this works: Under the equivalence conditions stated above, averaged per-example gradients are additive. Each accelerator first accumulates a local gradient over its 64 examples: \[ \frac{1}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{4} \left[\frac{1}{16}\sum_{i=1}^{16} \nabla \mathcal{L}(x_{ji})\right] \]

AllReduce then averages those local gradients across 8 accelerators, producing the global effective batch: \[ \frac{1}{8}\sum_{r=1}^{8} \nabla \mathcal{L}_{\text{local}}^{(r)}, \qquad B = 8 \times 64 = 512 \]

Operational comparison

Resident configuration:

  • 32 accelerators \(\times\) 16 sequences = 512 resident sequences per update
  • Gradient synchronization spans 32 accelerators
  • Hourly cost: $128/hour

Accumulated configuration:

  • 8 accelerators \(\times\) (16 sequences \(\times\) 4 micro-batches) = 512 sequences per update
  • Gradient synchronization occurs once per update across 8 accelerators, all within one server
  • Hourly cost: $32/hour
  • Hourly reduction: $96/hour, or 75 percent

Quality gate

  • Match the examples and their order, loss normalization, optimizer state and step schedule, and stochastic-layer behavior.
  • Compare held-out loss or perplexity and the optimization trajectory across repeated runs rather than assuming that the effective batch alone guarantees equivalent quality.
  • Expect small round-off differences from the changed summation order; require convergence within a declared tolerance rather than bitwise identity. No perplexity result is assumed here.

Systems insight: Accumulation lowers accelerator count and hourly burn, but ideal scaling preserves accelerator-hours; actual cost depends on utilization and communication.

Listing 8 places one AllReduce after 4 local micro-batches, making the synchronization boundary explicit.

Practical considerations

Gradient accumulation is most valuable when optimal batch sizes exceed GPU memory capacity. Transformer language models often use effective batches of hundreds of thousands to millions of tokens; if memory only permits a smaller number of sequences per device, accumulation bridges the gap without requiring additional hardware. Activation checkpointing complements this by enabling deeper architectures and is commonly used in transformer-scale training.

Both techniques introduce explicit trade-offs. Activation checkpointing recomputes discarded activations during backward propagation; with ordinary segment checkpointing, each discarded activation is typically reconstructed once, while recursive schedules may behave differently. Gradient accumulation reduces parameter-update frequency because each update follows \(\AccumulationStepCount\) micro-batches. When each micro-batch loss is divided by \(\AccumulationStepCount\), the accumulated gradient follows the effective-batch mean convention. If losses are summed instead, the gradients must be rescaled before the optimizer step or the learning-rate convention changed accordingly. Framework and codebase conventions differ, making normalization a common source of subtle bugs.

8.6.6 GPT-2 optimization walkthrough

Training GPT-2 XL at batch 32 on a single H100 exposes the physical boundaries that govern large-model optimization. The tensor allocation first exceeds accelerator memory, and once it fits, a naive input pipeline starves the execution engines. Each step below fixes whatever blocks the next, memory feasibility first and execution evidence second, before any accelerator is added.

Napkin Math 8.10: GPT-2 optimization on one H100
Step 1: Establish the baseline memory footprint.

The trace starts with GPT-2 XL at batch 32 and sequence length 1,024, FP32 tensors throughout, standard attention, and a single-threaded synchronous data loader. Weights, gradients, and Adam moments take 24 GB, and FP32 activations take 574 GB, so the step needs 598 GB, about 7× the H100’s 80 GB (85.9 GB in decimal units). The run fails the machine constraint before throughput matters.

Step 2: Apply mixed precision.

The first intervention targets memory feasibility rather than speed. Mixed precision (notebook 8.6) halves the activations and leaves the static state unchanged, reducing the step to 311 GB. The allocation still exceeds the accelerator.

Step 3: Replace standard attention with FlashAttention.

Never storing the quadratic score matrices removes most of the remaining activations and brings the step to 109.6 GB, still above capacity. With this kernel, the largest batch that stays resident is about 23 sequences, so the configuration overflows somewhere between batch 16 and batch 32.

Step 4: Fit the batch.

Two techniques close the gap. Accumulating two micro-batches of 16 keeps the same arithmetic per update and needs 66.8 GB. Segment checkpointing (notebook 8.8) keeps all 32 sequences resident in 35.5 GB but adds about 33 percent more arithmetic per step. The walkthrough takes accumulation, because the batch it needs fits without recomputation; checkpointing is the choice when a larger resident micro-batch matters more than arithmetic. Runtime measurement must still verify that backward workspaces, output and loss buffers, and allocator overhead fit in the remaining capacity.

Step 5: Read the profile.

Fitting the model resolves capacity but does not guarantee efficient execution. With single-core tokenization, host preparation takes longer than the device’s step (example 8.1), so a serial pipeline keeps the accelerator busy only about 48 percent of the time (equation 7). The diagnostic focus shifts from machine memory capacity (M) to the data pipeline (D) in D·A·M terms: reducing accelerator memory further would not touch the dominant exposed delay.

Step 6: Apply prefetching and parallel preparation.

Eight worker processes, page-locked (pinned) host memory for asynchronous DMA transfers, and a prefetch queue overlap host preparation with accelerator computation, and the parallel preparation interval falls well inside the device’s step. In the ideal overlapped model the accelerator is then busy for the whole step. A busy accelerator is not yet an efficient one; MFU measures how much of that busy time does useful model arithmetic, and re-profiling must establish whether memory bandwidth, arithmetic throughput, or another stage now limits execution.

Result: At the assumed 50 percent utilization, one step takes 0.60 s, the accelerator processes about 55,000 tokens/s, and the run takes about 35 days on one accelerator (about 47 days with segment checkpointing at the same executed rate). Table 15 collects the result.

Systems insight: The order of interventions follows a dependency, not a preference. Memory feasibility comes first because a configuration that does not fit produces no profile at all. Once it runs, the profile decides what comes next; in this scenario exposed input waits made the data pipeline the target ahead of any kernel tuning, because a faster kernel cannot shorten a step spent waiting for its input. After each intervention, re-profiling determines whether input delivery, memory bandwidth, arithmetic throughput, or another execution cost now limits progress.

This walkthrough demonstrates why training optimization is a sequential diagnostic discipline rather than an arbitrary collection of techniques. Each intervention addresses a constraint established by the memory calculation or the profile. Mixed precision halves activation storage, FlashAttention removes the quadratic attention term, and accumulation brings the batch within capacity without extra arithmetic, a 9× modeled memory reduction in all. Prefetching then addresses exposed input waits once execution begins. The result is a run of about 35 days on one accelerator.

Checkpoint 8.3: From a feasible configuration to a validated run

The walkthrough combines memory calculations with a modeled execution profile. Use the distinction to decide what an actual run must verify.

Single-accelerator optimizations address complementary constraints: mixed precision enables higher-throughput arithmetic, FlashAttention avoids materializing full attention matrices in HBM, accumulation and checkpointing bound activation memory, and asynchronous prefetching hides host input latency. Even fully optimized, however, a run of GPT-2 XL’s size occupies one accelerator for about 35 days, and larger runs are bounded by the arithmetic throughput of an individual chip.

Scaling beyond single-device compute or memory capacity requires distributing computation across multiple accelerators. This architectural transition introduces inter-device communication overhead, collective synchronization barriers, and fault-tolerance constraints that become the primary determinants of scaling efficiency.

Self-Check: Question

  1. For each batch, a training step has three dependent stages in a serial baseline: data preprocessing (\(T_{\text{prep}} = 30\text{ ms}\)), host-to-device transfer (\(T_{\text{xfer}} = 10\text{ ms}\)), and GPU compute (\(T_{\text{comp}} = 50\text{ ms}\)). Different batches can occupy independent resources and overlap with correct handoff. If the team implements steady-state overlapping with prefetching and double buffering, what iteration interval is theoretically possible after fill and before drain?

    1. \(40\text{ ms}\), because preprocessing and transfer replace compute once overlap is enabled.
    2. \(50\text{ ms}\), because in ideal steady state the interval is bounded by the slowest overlapped stage rather than the serial sum.
    3. \(10\text{ ms}\), because all stages run concurrently and the fastest one sets the pace.
    4. \(90\text{ ms}\), because DMA transfers cannot overlap useful GPU computation.

  2. Explain how FlashAttention computes exact self-attention with substantially lower memory footprint than standard attention, and describe what its backward pass does instead of reading a full stored score matrix from HBM.

  3. Why does the chapter’s conventional FP16 mixed-precision recipe keep FP32 master weights and perform selected reductions in FP32 instead of forcing every part of training into 16-bit precision?

    1. Tensor Cores cannot execute backpropagation unless gradients are stored in FP64.
    2. FP16 allocations can lock the accelerator memory bus unless the master weights are moved to host RAM.
    3. Automatic differentiation requires symbolic derivatives to be represented only in FP32.
    4. Very small parameter updates can be rounded away when applied directly to FP16 weights, and selected reductions benefit from the wider range or precision of FP32 accumulation.

  4. Gradient accumulation increases the memory footprint on the accelerator linearly with the accumulation factor \(K\) because all \(K\) micro-batches’ intermediate activations must remain in GPU memory simultaneously until the optimizer step.

  5. In FP16 mixed-precision training, the loss is multiplied before backpropagation so the resulting gradients are shifted into a safer numeric range, then divided back down before the optimizer update. This technique is known as loss ____.

  6. A team enables mixed precision on a transformer model and observes compute time drop by 45 percent, but total step time decreases by only 10 percent because the GPU now shows large idle gaps. Explain what likely happened in the system pipeline and what the team should do next.

See Answers →

8.7 Scaling Training Systems

The GPT-2 walkthrough fits the batch-32 step on one H100 and finishes the run in about 35 days. One accelerator cannot finish sooner, and some models cannot fit at all. Spread across the 8 accelerators of one server with perfect scaling, the same run would take about 4.4 days. A 70-billion-parameter model needs about 140 GB for its FP16 weights alone and far more for full training state, which no single accelerator holds, motivating sharding, offload, or multiple devices.

Two micro-batches of 16 fit one H100; batch 32 without checkpointing and a 70B model do not.

When a chosen workload still exceeds one device’s memory or schedule, spreading it across devices provides aggregate memory and compute. Other responses include reducing the model, batch, sequence length, or training scope; offloading state; and parameter-efficient adaptation, which trains only a small set of added parameters on top of a frozen model. Scaling beyond one device begins with multi-GPU configurations inside one machine and may extend across nodes. The major parallelism strategies make different communication trade-offs; a full treatment of distributed-training implementation lies beyond this chapter.

Not all workloads benefit equally from adding GPUs. Adding accelerators helps only until communication and synchronization consume the time saved. Figure 15 charts this scaling decay on a log-log speedup plot up to 256 GPUs, contrasting ideal linear scaling against compute-bound (ResNet), balanced (a large language model (LLM) over NVLink), and communication-bound regimes. The widening divergence between the ideal linear dashed reference and the plateauing modeled curves illustrates the communication overhead that accumulates as synchronization compounds. In this fixed-fraction model, even unlimited parallel compute leaves the non-scaling fraction of the original runtime, so the limiting speedup is its reciprocal, the Amdahl bound of equation 11.1.

The red curve’s widening gap from the dashed ideal is that communication overhead made visible, and shrinking that gap is what the distributed strategies in section 8.7.1 exist to do. Those strategies are older than deep learning. Training platforms inherit dense matrix multiplication kernels and tightly synchronized parallel execution from high-performance computing (HPC). From warehouse-scale computing they inherit cluster orchestration and automated fault recovery for multi-day jobs, the lineage that matters once a run spans many machines.

The same inheritance appears inside one training system as pressure at three scales. At data scale, large corpora require high-throughput input pipelines to avoid accelerator starvation. At model scale, billion-parameter architectures force a choice between replicating weights across devices and partitioning the model. At infrastructure scale, coordinating multiple accelerators introduces synchronization overhead that can dominate training time. The stage interfaces formalized in Stage interface contracts govern what each scale may assume of the others.

8.7.1 Single-node multi-GPU training

Multi-GPU training within a single node introduces the core parallelism strategies. AlexNet16 (Krizhevsky et al. 2012) split its convolutional layers across two GTX 580 GPUs because the 3 GB device memory limit prevented single-chip execution, making cross-GPU connectivity an immediate architectural constraint. Confining multi-accelerator topologies to single-node interconnects (such as PCIe or NVLink) isolates these strategies before cross-rack network fabrics enter.

16 AlexNet (2012): The paper reports 60M parameters and a 3 GB single-GPU ceiling, but does not attribute the limit to one memory component. Its model-parallel design placed roughly half the kernels on each GPU and allowed communication only in selected layers, explicitly trading memory capacity against communication.

Krizhevsky, Alex, Ilya Sutskever, and Geoffrey E. Hinton. 2012. “ImageNet Classification with Deep Convolutional Neural Networks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 25.

The two foundational strategies partition different things. Data parallelism replicates the model and partitions the data, while model parallelism partitions model state or computation. This distinction determines memory requirements, communication patterns, and scaling behavior.

Data parallelism

Data parallelism replicates the entire model on each GPU, with each processing different batches. After computing gradients locally, GPUs synchronize via gradient averaging. Input batches split across parallel GPU workers for independent forward and backward passes, then converge into an AllReduce collective, which sums the gradients from every worker and returns the result to each of them, so every replica applies the identical parameter update (figure 16).

When a model and its optimizer state fit comfortably within single-device memory, data parallelism serves as the primary scaling baseline because local forward and backward kernels run at native memory bandwidth without inter-device synchronization. Gradient aggregation is the principal recurring communication cost; some implementations also synchronize buffers or other state. Historically, frameworks supported multi-GPU execution within a single node using multithreaded wrappers like PyTorch’s DataParallel (DP). However, this single-process design suffers from two severe architectural bottlenecks. The Python runtime remains throttled by the Global Interpreter Lock (GIL), and a primary coordinator (GPU 0) must sequentially scatter mini-batches and gather outputs over PCIe, saturating host-device interconnect bandwidth and creating severe compute and memory imbalances. Modern systems commonly use PyTorch’s DistributedDataParallel (DDP); TensorFlow’s MirroredStrategy also provides replicated training but has a different execution model. With an independent Python process per accelerator, DDP avoids cross-replica contention for one Python GIL. Each process maintains its own model replica and local optimizer, relying on decentralized collective AllReduce operations that bucket gradients into contiguous buffers and overlap synchronization directly with backward-pass computation.

Synchronizing gradients is the step that data parallelism adds, and for GPT-2 XL inside one server its cost can be derived directly. Each replica holds FP16 gradients of 3 GB, and every replica must end the step with their sum. Ring AllReduce (Patarasuk and Yuan 2009) arranges the \(\AcceleratorCount\) accelerators in a logical ring and splits the gradient into \(\AcceleratorCount\) chunks. In a reduce-scatter phase, each accelerator passes a chunk to its neighbor \(\AcceleratorCount - 1\) times, adding the chunk it receives to its own, until each holds one fully summed chunk. In an all-gather phase, the summed chunks circulate \(\AcceleratorCount - 1\) more times until every accelerator holds all of them. Each accelerator therefore sends \(2(\AcceleratorCount-1)/\AcceleratorCount\) of the gradient bytes \(\DataVolume\), nearly twice the gradient however large the ring, and over a link of bandwidth \(\Bandwidth\) the bandwidth term of the synchronization time is

Patarasuk, Pitch, and Xin Yuan. 2009. “Bandwidth Optimal All-Reduce Algorithms for Clusters of Workstations.” Journal of Parallel and Distributed Computing 69 (2): 117–24. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jpdc.2008.09.002.

\[ \CommunicationTime \approx \frac{2(\AcceleratorCount-1)}{\AcceleratorCount} \cdot \frac{\DataVolume}{\Bandwidth} \tag{12}\]

For 8 H100s the factor is 1.75, so each accelerator sends 5.25 GB per step, which takes about 12 ms at 450 GB/s per direction over NVLink. Against a 596 ms step when each replica processes the full batch of 32 sequences, synchronization is about 2 percent of the step even without overlap. Splitting a fixed batch of 32 eight ways shrinks each replica’s compute to 75 ms while the gradient bytes stay the same, raising the unhidden share to about 14 percent. Data parallelism inside one server is cheap for GPT-2 XL as long as each replica keeps enough work to amortize a synchronization whose size depends only on the parameter count. Frameworks hide most of what remains by launching bucketed AllReduce calls while the backward pass is still computing earlier layers’ gradients.

Unsharded data parallelism has a hard constraint. Every accelerator holds a complete model replica and its full training state, 16 bytes per parameter in the mixed-precision Adam recipe, or 24 GB for GPT-2 XL on each of the 8 accelerators. The Zero Redundancy Optimizer (ZeRO) (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) removes that redundancy by sharding training state across the data-parallel replicas in three stages. ZeRO-1 shards the 12 bytes of FP32 master weights and Adam moments, leaving 8.2 GB per accelerator for GPT-2 XL. ZeRO-2 also shards the gradients, leaving 5.6 GB. ZeRO-3, which fully sharded data parallelism (FSDP) (Zhao et al. 2023) implements, also shards the FP16 parameters, leaving 3 GB. The same arithmetic brings the Llama 3 8B full fine-tune that overflowed one accelerator in section 8.3.3.3 down to about 16 GB of static state per accelerator. That memory saving rearranges how replicas communicate. Recall that one Ring AllReduce consists of a reduce-scatter phase followed by an all-gather phase, moving two parameter-sized tensors per replica in total. ZeRO-1 and ZeRO-2 split those same two phases around the local optimizer step at the same total volume: a reduce-scatter delivers to each accelerator only the summed gradients for the parameter shard whose optimizer state it owns, each accelerator updates its shard locally, and an all-gather broadcasts the updated FP16 weights back to every replica. ZeRO-3 shards the FP16 weights as well and discards non-local weight shards after each layer uses them. It must therefore all-gather each layer’s weights once for the forward pass and a second time for the backward pass before reduce-scattering the layer’s gradients, which requires three parameter-sized collective passes instead of two, or about 1.5 times the data-parallel communication volume (Rajbhandari et al. 2020). A model that still exceeds local memory after sharding requires pipeline or tensor partitioning.

Rajbhandari, Samyam, Jeff Rasley, Olatunji Ruwase, and Yuxiong He. 2020. “ZeRO: Memory Optimizations Toward Training Trillion Parameter Models.” SC20: International Conference for High Performance Computing, Networking, Storage and Analysis, 1–16. https://doi.org/10.1109/sc41405.2020.00024.
Zhao, Yanli, Andrew Gu, Rohan Varma, Liang Luo, Chien-Chin Huang, Min Xu, Less Wright, et al. 2023. “PyTorch FSDP: Experiences on Scaling Fully Sharded Data Parallel.” Proceedings of the VLDB Endowment 16 (12): 3848–60. https://doi.org/10.14778/3611540.3611569.

Model parallelism

Model parallelism partitions the model itself across GPUs, which becomes necessary when the model exceeds single-GPU memory. AlexNet adopted an early form of this strategy: selected convolutional layers split their kernels and channels across two GPUs, with communication at selected connections. Figure 17 illustrates a different, layerwise partition of the same broader model-parallel idea: Device 1 through Device 3 sequentially process layers 1–16, 17–32, and 33–48, streaming boundary activations forward during the forward pass and propagating loss gradients in reverse during backpropagation.

Systems Perspective 8.4: Data vs. model parallelism
When a selected workload is partitioned across \(\AcceleratorCount\) devices, the split may divide examples, model state, layers, or tensor operations. For a model with parameters \(\Parameters\) and batch size \(\BatchSize\), data and model parallelism provide two starting points.

Data parallelism (split the batch): For a fixed global batch, each of \(\AcceleratorCount\) workers processes about \(1/\AcceleratorCount\) of the examples, but every worker holds a model replica and ordinarily its associated training state. Dense gradient communication is proportional to \(\Parameters\) per update. Scaling departs from the ideal as local batches shrink or synchronization and input costs grow.

Model parallelism (split model computation): Each accelerator stores and computes a portion of the model. An even partition may approach \(\Parameters/\AcceleratorCount\) parameters per worker, but embeddings, buffers, imbalance, and replicated state alter that estimate. Communication depends on the partition: layerwise pipeline parallelism exchanges activations at stage boundaries, while tensor parallelism performs collectives within partitioned layers.

Systems insight: Data parallelism is the simplest candidate when the complete training state fits per worker and more throughput is needed. Model-state, pipeline, or tensor partitioning becomes necessary when it does not, often in combination with data parallelism.

When a model exceeds the memory capacity of a single accelerator, its depth can be partitioned across sequential devices. Figure 18 stages this pipeline allocation across a four-accelerator execution chain, grouping 24 transformer blocks into contiguous six-block partitions (Blocks 1–6 through 19–24) that exchange boundary activations over the interconnect.

The main bottleneck in naive layer-wise partitioning is device serialization. Because layer evaluation is strictly sequential, Device 1 computes layers 1–6 while Devices 2–4 sit idle waiting for forward activations. During the backward pass, the dependency reverses: Device 4 computes first while upstream devices wait for returning gradients. This idle duration constitutes the pipeline bubble, which caps theoretical hardware utilization at \(1/\AcceleratorCount\) under naive sequential execution. It differs from the accelerator bubbles of the staged training pipeline, which arise when one device’s data-loading, transfer, and compute stages run at mismatched rates. A pipeline bubble arises even with perfectly fed devices, because each device must wait for another device’s layers. Mitigating this idle time requires either pipelining micro-batches across stages or partitioning tensor dimensions within layers so that all devices compute simultaneously.

Both remedies fit inside one node. Pipeline parallelism splits each batch into micro-batches and streams them through the stages, so Device 2 works on the first micro-batch while Device 1 starts the second. The bubble then shrinks to the time spent filling and draining the pipeline at the start and end of each batch (Huang et al. 2019; Narayanan et al. 2019). Tensor parallelism instead splits the matrix operations of each layer across devices, so every device works on every layer at once (Shoeybi et al. 2019).
